### 1. The intuition: why one agent makes six shapes

⚠️ A river makes essentially one kind of lake — an ox-bow — and it makes it one way. ⚠️ Wind makes essentially one — a deflation hollow. ⚠️ Ice makes **six**, and the reason is that ice does three things no other agent does simultaneously.

❗ **First, ice both erodes and deposits, in the same system, at the same time.** ⚠️ A river in flood erodes; a river in retreat deposits; but they are different moments. ⚠️ A glacier is **plucking and abrading at its base while dumping at its snout and sides continuously**, so at any instant the valley contains both an erosional trough and a depositional ridge. ❗ Half the glacial lake types are erosional and half are depositional.

❗ **Second, ice can erode unevenly along its own path, and it can erode below the level of its own exit.** ⚠️ Water cannot: a river's bed must slope downhill all the way or the water stops. ⚠️ Ice is a solid under pressure; it can be **pushed up and over a rise** by the ice behind it. ⚠️ Therefore a glacier can gouge a closed hollow in mid-valley — an **overdeepening** — and still keep flowing out of it. ❗ That is why glacial troughs have **rock basins** in them and river valleys do not.

❗ **Third, ice leaves behind blocks of itself.** ⚠️ No other agent does this. When a glacier retreats, lumps of stagnant ice detach, get buried in outwash sand and gravel, and then melt — years or decades later — leaving a **void** where the ice was. ⚠️ The sediment above collapses in, and a hollow appears in a surface that was flat when it was deposited. ❗ **That is a kettle, and it is a hole produced by the disappearance of material rather than by the removal of rock.**

> 🔑 **Mnemonic for the six, in transect order:** **T-R-M-K-D**, plus **R** off-transect — **T**arn (cirque), **R**ibbon (trough), **M**orainic-dammed (behind the ridge), **K**ettle (in the outwash), **D**rift-deposition (between drumlins), and **R**ock-hollow (on the shield, no valley at all).

### 2. The six sub-types, each with mechanism, geometry and change behaviour

❗ **The lesson contract for this lesson is the six, each correctly matched to its process. The rubric allocates six of its ten marks to exactly that, which is why each gets its own paragraph rather than a row in a list.**

**(1) Tarn — erosional, bedrock, in a cirque.** ✅ Leong, printed 67: cirque and tarn lakes, with **Red Tarn** named and the photograph captioned *"Blea Water in **Westmorland**"* — ❗ [N-13] **Cumbria** in current administrative naming. ⚠️ **Mechanism:** snow accumulates in a hollow on a shaded slope, compacts to ice, and the ice body **rotates** as it moves, scouring the floor of the hollow deeper while the ice at the downhill edge is thinner and scours less. ⚠️ The result is an **armchair-shaped bedrock basin with a rock lip** on its open side. ⚠️ **Geometry:** small in area, **circular to semicircular**, surprisingly deep for its size, steep-sided on three sides, with a low lip on the fourth. ⚠️ **Change behaviour:** very small catchment, so very little sediment arrives; floored on bedrock, so little seepage; and cold, so little biological production. ❗ **Tarns are among the longest-lived small lakes on Earth** — the opposite of the ox-bow, which is their counterpart in size and nothing else.

**(2) Ribbon lake — erosional, bedrock, in the trough.** ✅ Leong names **Ullswater**. ⚠️ **Mechanism:** the trunk glacier erodes the valley floor **unevenly**, cutting deeper where the rock is weaker, where a tributary glacier added ice and so added erosive power, or where the valley narrowed and the ice thickened. ⚠️ These **overdeepenings** become rock basins; when the ice leaves, they hold water. ⚠️ **Geometry:** ❗ **long, narrow and aligned with the valley** — the single most reliable identification. Often several in a chain down one valley, separated by rock bars. ⚠️ **Change behaviour:** a large catchment feeds it, so sediment arrives steadily, and a ribbon lake is progressively infilled from its upper end by a delta building into it. ❗ **The classic field evidence is a lake whose head is a flat alluvial plain** — that plain is the part of the lake that has already been filled.

**(3) Morainic-dammed lake — depositional dam, erosional trough.** ✅ Leong names **Windermere**. ⚠️ **Mechanism:** a terminal or recessional moraine — the ridge of unsorted debris dumped at a stationary ice front — lies **across** the valley, and water ponds behind it. ⚠️ **Geometry:** follows the valley like a ribbon lake, but the **dam is at the downstream end and is made of loose material**, not rock. ⚠️ **Change behaviour, and it is the important one:** ❗ **a moraine dam is unconsolidated, poorly sorted and often contains buried ice.** ⚠️ It can be **overtopped, piped through or breached**, and when it fails it fails **suddenly and completely**, releasing the whole lake. 🔁 ❗ **That failure chain — its triggers, its inventory, its risk assessment and its mitigation — is glacial-lake outburst-flood material and belongs in full to `Geography` Topic 06, `06-Landforms-of-Glaciation-Himalayan-Glaciers-GLOF`, which is released at 19 lessons.** ❗ **This lesson states that the dam is weak and stops there.** ⚠️ What Topic 09 contributes to that hazard literature is the **lake side** of it: the volume impounded, the residence time, and the fact that a morainic dam is the only lake dam in the natural world that routinely contains ice within the dam itself.

**(4) Kettle lake — depositional void.** ✅ Leong names *"the meres of Shropshire"* and Orkney. ⚠️ **Mechanism:** a detached block of stagnant ice is **buried** by outwash sand and gravel; it melts out over years to decades; the overlying sediment collapses into the void; a closed hollow appears. ⚠️ **Geometry:** ❗ **small, roughly circular to irregular, shallow, and in a flat or gently undulating depositional surface** — which is the tell, because nothing else makes a hole in a plain. ⚠️ Often occurring in **groups**, because ice blocks detach in numbers, producing the "kettle-and-kame" topography. ⚠️ **Change behaviour:** shallow and set in permeable outwash, so a kettle is vulnerable to **seepage loss** as well as infilling; many become bogs rather than open water.

❗ **The tarn-against-kettle discrimination, which the canonical trap list singles out, in one line each.** ⚠️ **Tarn:** a hollow **cut into solid rock** by moving ice, high in a mountain, with steep rock walls. ⚠️ **Kettle:** a hollow **in loose sediment** produced by ice **melting away**, low on a plain, with gentle sandy margins. ❗ They share only the agent. ❗ A candidate who can state that one is erosional-in-bedrock and the other depositional-in-drift has the distinction permanently.

**(5) Drift-deposition lake — deranged drainage in uneven drift.** ✅ Leong, PDF 71, names the drumlin landscape of **County Down**. ⚠️ **Mechanism:** an ice sheet lays down **till unevenly**, moulding it into mounds — drumlins — or simply dumping it in irregular sheets. ⚠️ The pre-glacial drainage lines are **buried and destroyed**, and the post-glacial surface has no organised valleys, so water collects in whatever hollows exist between the mounds. ⚠️ **Geometry:** irregular, elongated **parallel to the drumlin long axes** where drumlins are present, shallow, numerous. ⚠️ ❗ **This is the signature of "deranged drainage"**, and the lake pattern itself is the evidence. ⚠️ **Change behaviour:** shallow and sediment-fed; these are among the first glacial lakes to become marsh.

**(6) Rock-hollow lake — ice-sheet scour on a lowland shield.** ✅ Leong, printed 67: **Finland**, *"Suomi, Land of Lakes"*, *"over 35,000 glacial lakes"*. ⚠️ **Mechanism:** a continental ice sheet, rather than a valley glacier, moves across a **low, hard, jointed crystalline surface**, plucking blocks out along joints and weak zones and scouring irregular shallow basins over an enormous area. ⚠️ **Geometry:** ❗ **thousands of small, shallow, irregular, interconnected basins with no valley organisation at all** — the opposite of the ribbon lake's valley alignment. ⚠️ **Change behaviour:** shallow and set on impermeable crystalline rock, so they persist hydrologically but fill biologically — peat and organic sediment rather than mineral silt.

> ❗ **[U-04] A source flag, not a correction.** ✅ Leong's *"over 35,000 glacial lakes"* for Finland is quoted **with the book attributed**. ⚠️ Commonly cited counts for Finnish lakes are **far higher** under smaller size thresholds — lake counts are **entirely threshold-dependent**, since lowering the minimum area multiplies the count. ❗ **The discrepancy is flagged here and is not adjudicated**, because the threshold Leong used is not stated on the page. ❗ **No alternative count is asserted.**

### 3. [A3] applied — why glacial lakes are deep for their area but not deep absolutely

⚠️ Lesson 3 established that each process has a characteristic vertical scale. ❗ Glacial overdeepening sits in the **middle** of that range — hundreds of metres, not kilometres — and the reason is instructive.

```text
   WHAT LIMITS GLACIAL OVERDEEPENING

   Ice can flow UP a reverse slope only while the ice BEHIND it is
   thick enough to push it. The driving force is the ice-surface
   gradient; the resisting force rises with the height of the
   reverse slope.

      ice surface  ________
                 /         \_______
      ========= /                   \======== ice surface gradient
       \       /                      \         drives the flow
        \     /   OVERDEEPENING        \
         \___/                          \____
             ^
             |  the deeper this gets, the STEEPER the reverse slope
             |  the ice must climb to leave, and the more of the
             |  driving stress is consumed doing so.

   ==> A GLACIER OVERDEEPENS UNTIL THE REVERSE SLOPE CONSUMES THE
       AVAILABLE DRIVING STRESS. That is a SELF-LIMITING process,
       and the limit scales with ICE THICKNESS -- hundreds of
       metres, not kilometres.

   CONTRAST: tectonic subsidence has NO self-limiting term at all.
   The block drops for as long as extension continues.

   ===> GLACIAL LAKES ARE DEEP FOR THEIR AREA, AND SHALLOW COMPARED
        WITH TECTONIC LAKES. Both halves of that sentence matter.
```

⚠️ **The consequence for the change argument.** ⚠️ A glacial lake typically has a **low to moderate area-to-volume ratio** — better protected against evaporative loss than a playa, worse than a rift lake. ⚠️ Its residence time is usually **long in relation to its small catchment**, which makes it **more vulnerable to nutrient loading than its clear water suggests**: a small amount of nutrient arriving at a small lake with a long residence time produces a high concentration. ❗ That is exactly why 🇮🇳 **Dal Lake**, a modified glacial-and-structural Himalayan lake with heavy local nutrient input, eutrophies, while much larger lakes with greater throughflow do not. ❗ That line is developed at Lessons 9 and 10.

### 4. 🇮🇳 India example — Tsongmo, Gangabal and Sheshnag, and the warning the canonical owner attaches

🇮🇳 ✅ **Tsongmo, Sikkim.** ✅ Majid, *Geography of India*, printed **3.31**, read off the rendered page: Tsongmo lies in Sikkim, **about 40 kilometres from Gangtok**, is described in the source as ***"a glaciated tarn lake"***, is **oval** in plan, stands at **about 3,780 metres**, and is sacred. ❗ **This is the single most valuable India sentence in the whole glacial section of the local corpus**, because it gives an Indian lake with an **explicit genetic label from a book**, not an inference — and the label is the first of the six types, with the geometry (oval, small, high) that the mechanism predicts.

🇮🇳 ✅ **Gangabal and Sheshnag, Kashmir Himalaya.** ✅ The canonical India owner lists **Gangabal**, **Sheshnag**, **Kailash-Kund** and **Sanasar** as **glacial** lakes, and states the regional rule plainly: ✅ *"glacial/Pleistocene lakes are confined to the **Himalaya**; the Peninsula has **no evidence of Pleistocene glaciation** in the extracted source."* ❗ That rule is the basis of the canonical trap: ✅ *"Do not transfer Himalayan glacial-lake logic to Peninsular India; Peninsular lakes are mainly lagoonal, tectonic/crater, tank/reservoir, ox-bow or salt-lake types."*

🇮🇳 ✅ **Suraj Tal.** ✅ Majid, printed 3.31: below the **Baralacha Pass**, at about **4,980 metres**, the source of the **Bhaga**, a main branch of the Chenab. ❗ Its altitude is the evidence for correction **[N-03]** stated at Lesson 3 — Titicaca at roughly 3,800 metres is not the highest lake in the world when an Indian lake sits 1,200 metres above it.

❗ **The warning the canonical owner attaches, and it is the most important sentence in the India half of this lesson.** ✅ *"Do not classify every lower Kumaun lake as purely glacial: **faulting, structural control, landslides and depositional blockage may interact**."* ⚠️ The owner applies this specifically to **Naini**, which it classes as a *"tectonic/structural basin, modified by slope processes"*, and to **Sat Tal**, whose origin it calls *"complex"*. ❗ **This is a compound-origin instruction, and it is the single most common place where candidates over-apply a label.** ⚠️ The correct posture for a Kumaun or lower-Himalayan lake is: **altitude is not a genetic argument.** A lake at 1,400 metres in the Lesser Himalaya is not glacial merely because it is in the mountains; the Pleistocene snowline was far higher than that for most of the range, and faulting, landslide damming and valley blockage are at least as likely.

```text
   THE INDIAN GLACIAL-LAKE DECISION PROCEDURE
   (built from the canonical owner's own warning; use it before applying
   ANY glacial label to an Indian lake)

   Q1. Is it in the HIMALAYA / extra-peninsular belt?
        NO  -> it is NOT glacial. The canonical owner is explicit: the
               Peninsula shows NO evidence of Pleistocene glaciation.
               Go to L14 and classify it as lagoon / crater / impact /
               tank / ox-bow / saline.
        YES -> Q2

   Q2. Is it HIGH -- near or above the former snowline, in or below
       a CIRQUE, in a GLACIATED TROUGH, or behind a MORAINE?
        YES -> a glacial label is DEFENSIBLE. Now say WHICH of the six:
               cirque hollow        -> TARN           (Tsongmo: the
               valley trough        -> RIBBON          source's own label)
               behind a moraine     -> MORAINIC-DAMMED
        NO / UNCERTAIN -> Q3

   Q3. Is it in the LESSER HIMALAYA / Kumaun at moderate altitude?
        -> DO NOT ASSERT A GLACIAL ORIGIN.
           The canonical owner names FOUR competing mechanisms:
           faulting | structural control | landslide | depositional
           blockage.
           Naini  = "tectonic/structural basin, modified by slope
                     processes"  (the owner's own classification)
           Sat Tal = origin "complex"; a GROUP of seven lakes near
                     Bhimtal at about 1,370 m (Majid printed 3.31)
        ==> THE DEFENSIBLE RESPONSE IS TO NAME THE COMPETING MECHANISMS,
            NOT TO PICK ONE WITHOUT EVIDENCE.
```

❗ **The limitation, stated rather than skipped.** ⚠️ Beyond Tsongmo, **no local book in this edition's evidence base supplies a genetic label for any named Indian Himalayan lake.** ❗ The Gangabal, Sheshnag, Kailash-Kund and Sanasar classifications are carried from the **canonical Advanced owner**, which is controlling for India scope, and are **not independently corroborated in any book**. ❗ **No depth, area, volume or altitude figure for Gangabal or Sheshnag appears anywhere in this edition**, because none is available to it.

### 5. The change consequence — what a glacial origin implies for how the lake will die

❗ [R-01] The genesis lesson closes on change.

| Sub-type | Dominant death mode | Timescale implied | The reason, in one line |
|---|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ **Tarn** | Slow **infill**, mostly organic | ❗ Millennia | Tiny catchment, bedrock floor, cold water, minimal sediment supply |
| ⚠️ **Ribbon** | **Infill by a delta** from the upper end | ⚠️ Centuries to millennia | Large catchment delivering sediment into a narrow basin; the flat valley head is the already-filled part |
| ❗ **Morainic-dammed** | ❗ **Sudden, total loss by dam failure** | ❗ **Instantaneous, at any time** | The dam is unconsolidated debris, sometimes ice-cored. 🔁 **The hazard chain belongs to Topic 06** |
| ⚠️ **Kettle** | **Seepage plus infill**, often to bog | ⚠️ Decades to centuries | Shallow, set in permeable outwash, small volume |
| ⚠️ **Drift-deposition** | **Infill to marsh** | ⚠️ Centuries | Shallow and sediment-fed in a disorganised drainage |
| ⚠️ **Rock-hollow** | **Organic infill**, not mineral | ⚠️ Millennia | Impermeable crystalline floor; little mineral sediment; peat accumulation dominates |

❗ **The analytically important row is the third, and it is the row this edition hands off.** ⚠️ Every other glacial lake type dies **gradually**, by one of Leong's two terminations operating slowly. ⚠️ The morainic-dammed lake is the one natural lake type whose characteristic death is **catastrophic and instantaneous**, because its dam is a pile of loose debris that can be breached. ❗ **That single structural fact is Topic 09's entire contribution to the glacial-lake hazard literature**, and 🔁 everything downstream of it — trigger mechanisms, inventories, expansion rates, early warning, risk ranking and mitigation engineering — **is routed to `Geography` Topic 06's released edition and is not duplicated, summarised or hinted at here.**

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | What it explains | What it does not explain |
|---|---|---|
| **Erosional against depositional split** | Why the six types fall into two families; why tarn and kettle are opposites despite sharing an agent | Which will occur in a given valley, which depends on the ice regime and the lithology |
| **The self-limiting overdeepening argument** | Why glacial lakes are deep for their area but shallow against tectonic lakes | Absolute depths, which require measurement |
| **The compound-origin warning** | Why Naini and Sat Tal resist a single label; why altitude is not a genetic argument | The actual origin of any specific lower-Himalayan lake, which no source here resolves |

**Strongest objection.** ⚠️ Teaching six sub-types is exactly the taxonomy bloat the handoff warns against. The examiner has never asked for six glacial lake types, and a Mains answer has no room for them. Three would do.

**Reply.** ❗ The objection would be correct if the six were taught as a list, and this lesson is built so that they cannot be. ⚠️ **First**, they are arranged on a **single transect**, so what is memorised is not six items but **one valley**. ⚠️ **Second**, each is attached to a **distinct mechanism and a distinct death mode**, so they do analytical work rather than occupying slots — and the death-mode column is what the mapped change clause requires. ❗ **Third and decisively**, the 2019 owned demand asks for **the common feature shared by three unfamiliar water bodies**, which is a demand to **infer a shared class from names alone**; a reader holding three fuzzy glacial categories cannot run that inference. ❗ What is **not** taught here is moraine genesis, ice dynamics or outburst floods, all routed to Topic 06 twice in this lesson.

⚠️ **Residual.** The **relative frequency** of the six types, globally or in India, is **not available** to this edition, and lake counts of the kind Leong quotes for Finland are **threshold-dependent to the point of being incomparable** across sources. ❗ **No count, frequency or proportion of glacial lake types is stated anywhere in this file**, and [U-04] is flagged rather than resolved.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** General Studies Paper I, the mapped change clause. ⚠️ The glacial family supplies the clearest case in the whole topic that **origin predicts mode of death** — five of the six die gradually and one dies instantly, and the difference is entirely in the dam.
- **Verified linkage:** ⚠️ No owned demand is answered by this lesson alone. ❗ It builds the **discrimination capability** behind **2019, Prelims GS-I, Q42**, which asks for the common feature shared by three unfamiliar names — ❗ **key unavailable locally, no answer recorded or inferred** — and behind **2018, Prelims GS-I, Q77**, on identifying an artificial lake among named Indian water bodies, since telling natural from artificial requires knowing what each natural class looks like. 🔁 All **glacial-lake outburst-flood** demands, present or future, belong to Topic 06.
- **Traps:** equating tarn with kettle; calling any mountain lake glacial; assuming altitude proves glaciation; transferring Himalayan glacial logic to the Peninsula; quoting Leong's Finnish lake count as a current or threshold-independent figure; importing GLOF content into a lake-classification answer.
- **Answer use:** ⚠️ For any glacial-lake question, lead with the **two-mode framing**, not with the list. ❗ *"Ice is the only agent that erodes and deposits simultaneously, so it produces both bedrock hollows — tarns in cirques, ribbon lakes in overdeepened troughs — and depositional forms: kettles where buried ice melts out of outwash, morainic-dammed lakes behind debris ridges, and deranged drainage lakes in uneven drift."* ⚠️ About **fifty-five words**, and it delivers five of the six types with their mechanisms attached.

Revision notes:

- ⚠️ **One agent, two modes, three settings, six forms.** ❗ Erode or deposit; cirque, trough, or beyond the ice front.
- ❗ **Tarn** = **erosional bedrock hollow** cut by **rotational** ice in a cirque, with a **rock lip**; small, circular, deep for its size. ✅ Leong: Red Tarn; *"Blea Water in Westmorland"* → ❗ [N-13] **Cumbria**.
- ❗ **Ribbon lake** = **overdeepening** in a glacial trough; ❗ **long, narrow, valley-aligned**, often in chains separated by rock bars. ✅ Leong: **Ullswater**.
- ❗ **Morainic-dammed** = **depositional dam across an erosional trough**. ✅ Leong: **Windermere**. ❗ The dam is **unconsolidated and sometimes ice-cored** → can fail **suddenly and totally**. 🔁 **Hazard chain routed entirely to Topic 06.**
- ❗ **Kettle** = **depositional void** where a **buried ice block melted out** of outwash; small, round, shallow, in a **flat plain**, usually in groups. ✅ Leong: *"the meres of Shropshire"*; Orkney.
- ❗ **Tarn against kettle, the canonical trap:** one is rock **removed** at altitude; the other is a hole **left behind** in sediment on a plain. They share only the agent.
- ⚠️ **Drift-deposition lake** = ponding in **deranged drainage** between uneven drift, elongated along drumlin axes. ✅ Leong: **County Down**.
- ⚠️ **Rock-hollow lake** = **ice-sheet** scour of a low crystalline shield; **thousands** of small, shallow, irregular basins with **no valley organisation**. ✅ Leong: Finland, *"Suomi, Land of Lakes"*, *"over 35,000 glacial lakes"*.
- ❗ **[U-04] flag:** that count is **threshold-dependent**; commonly cited Finnish figures are far higher at smaller thresholds. ❗ Quoted with the book attributed; **not adjudicated**.
- ⚠️ **[A3] Glacial overdeepening is self-limiting**: ice must climb the reverse slope to leave, and the steeper that slope, the more driving stress it consumes. ❗ Hundreds of metres, **not** kilometres — deep for the area, shallow against tectonic lakes.
- 🇮🇳 ✅ **Tsongmo**, Sikkim — about **40 km from Gangtok**, the source's **own label** *"a glaciated tarn lake"*, oval, about **3,780 m**, sacred (Majid, *Geography of India*, printed **3.31**).
- 🇮🇳 ✅ **Suraj Tal** — below the **Baralacha Pass**, about **4,980 m**, source of the **Bhaga**, a main branch of the Chenab (same page). ❗ Evidence for **[N-03]**.
- 🇮🇳 ✅ **Canonical glacial list:** Gangabal, Sheshnag, Kailash-Kund, Sanasar — all **Himalayan**. ✅ *"The Peninsula has no evidence of Pleistocene glaciation."*
- ❗ **The compound-origin warning:** ✅ *"faulting, structural control, landslides and depositional blockage may interact."* ❗ **Naini** is *"tectonic/structural ... modified by slope processes"*; **Sat Tal**'s origin is *"complex"* — a group of seven lakes near **Bhimtal** at about **1,370 m** (Majid printed 3.31). ❗ **Altitude is not a genetic argument.**
- ❗ **Change consequence:** five of six glacial types die **gradually** by infill or seepage; the **morainic-dammed** type is the one natural lake whose characteristic death is **instantaneous**, because its dam is loose debris. 🔁 Everything downstream of that fact is **Topic 06's**.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate's answer reads: "Glacial lakes include tarns and kettles, both formed by ice erosion in mountains. India's Himalayan lakes such as Gangabal, Sheshnag, Naini and Sat Tal are all glacial lakes, and Peninsular India also has glacial lakes at high altitude in the Western Ghats." Identify every error, and set out what a correct treatment of Indian glacial lakes would say instead.

**Model answer:** There are four errors of three kinds. **Error one, mechanism.** "Both formed by ice erosion" is wrong for the kettle and is the exact confusion the canonical trap names. A **tarn** is **erosional**: rotational ice movement in a cirque scours a basin **into bedrock** and leaves a rock lip, giving a small, circular, steep-sided hollow deep for its area. A **kettle** is **depositional**: a stagnant ice block is buried by outwash, melts out years later, and the overlying sediment collapses into the **void**, giving a shallow hollow in a flat depositional plain with sandy margins. One is rock taken away at altitude; the other is a hole left behind in sediment on a lowland. **Error two, setting.** Kettles form **beyond the ice front** in outwash, which is characteristically low-lying — the source's own examples are the Shropshire meres and Orkney. **Error three, over-application.** The canonical Advanced owner classes **Naini** as a *"tectonic/structural basin, modified by slope processes"*, calls **Sat Tal**'s origin *"complex"*, and warns that *"faulting, structural control, landslides and depositional blockage may interact"*. The underlying error is treating **altitude as a genetic argument**; naming the competing mechanisms scores higher than a confident single label. **Error four, region.** The owner states that *"the Peninsula has no evidence of Pleistocene glaciation"*. **A correct treatment** confines glacial lakes to the Himalaya and extra-peninsular belt — Gangabal, Sheshnag, Kailash-Kund and Sanasar on the owner's authority, and **Tsongmo**, which the book itself labels *"a glaciated tarn lake"* — states the four competing mechanisms for lower lakes, and keeps the **form** separate from the **hazard**, routing outburst-flood risk to the glaciation topic.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat a lake's position in a mountain range as evidence of a glacial origin. Mountains concentrate **every** lake-forming process at once — faulting, landslide damming, valley blockage, cirque scour and trough overdeepening — so altitude raises the prior for several mechanisms and discriminates between none. The discriminating evidence is the **form of the hollow and the material of its rim**.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "A single geomorphic agent, ice, produces lake basins by opposite mechanisms." Examine with examples, and state what this implies for how such lakes end. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** Ice erodes and deposits simultaneously, so it builds lake basins two opposite ways. Erosionally, rotational movement in a cirque scours a bedrock tarn with a rock lip — Leong's Red Tarn — while a trunk glacier overdeepens its trough into long, narrow ribbon lakes such as Ullswater, and an ice sheet scours a crystalline shield into the thousands of irregular rock hollows Leong records in Finland. Depositionally, a terminal moraine dams a trough to impound Windermere; buried ice blocks melt out of outwash to leave kettles such as the Shropshire meres; and uneven drift between drumlins in County Down derange drainage entirely. Tsongmo in Sikkim, at about 3,780 metres, is labelled a glaciated tarn by its source. The implication for endings is sharp: erosional and drift forms die gradually by infilling, but the morainic-dammed lake, held by unconsolidated and sometimes ice-cored debris, can fail suddenly and completely.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** **6** for the six glacial sub-types each correctly matched to its own process — one mark per correct type-and-mechanism pairing, with no mark for a type named without its mechanism; **2** for the India example with a sourced genetic label rather than an inferred one; **2** for the Topic 06 boundary stated explicitly, so that dam weakness is given as a lake property and the hazard chain is routed out. Deduct 2 for equating tarn with kettle; deduct 1 for asserting a glacial origin for a lower-Himalayan lake without naming the competing mechanisms.

## Lesson 5 — Genesis III: Volcanic lakes — crater, caldera, lava-dammed and subsidence

Progress: L5 / 16 — Genesis III: Volcanic lakes — crater, caldera, lava-dammed and subsidence  |  Stage: Core  |  Band: Standard

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Canonical basis: Basic owner §2, origin table rows 3 and 4 — *"Volcanic activity | Crater/caldera depression holds water | Crater / caldera lake | Lake Toba"* and, as a **separate row**, *"Impact | Meteorite impact excavates a crater | Impact-crater lake | Lonar"*; Basic owner §3.3 — *"Water may collect in a volcanic crater or larger caldera; Toba is a caldera-lake example. **Lonar** is a meteorite-impact lake excavated in Deccan basalt, **not** a volcanic crater"*; Basic owner's scale-and-map control clause, which requires that the *"Lonar impact basin"* retain its *"exact spatial/genetic qualifier"*.
Book basis: G.C. Leong, local scan PDF p.71 — Class 3, Volcanic, in three lettered parts: (a) crater and caldera lakes, with **Crater Lake, Oregon**, of which the text says it *"in fact occupies a **caldera**"*, together with **Toba** and **Avernus**, and **Figure 69**; (b) **lava-blocked** lakes, with the **Sea of Galilee**; (c) **volcanic subsidence** lakes, with **Myvatn**.
CA: 📰 Live search performed for this generation pass, 04 October 2026, for current material on volcanic and impact lakes bearing on **lake form**. ❗ Result: nothing first-party and dated was obtained that bears on crater-against-caldera morphology or on the classification of the Indian case. ❗ **No anchor is adopted for this lesson**, and none has been borrowed from a neighbouring lesson to disguise the gap. ⚠️ The reporting that does exist on the Indian case concerns **episodic colour change in the lake water**, which is a limnological-chemical phenomenon and is treated at Lesson 9 as a **hypersaline-alkaline stratification** question rather than being imported here as news.
Boundary: 🔁 **Topic 03 — Vulcanism and Earthquakes / India Seismic Zones, released, 16 lessons — owns eruption mechanics, magma-chamber behaviour, caldera-collapse dynamics and volcanic hazard in full.** ❗ This lesson teaches the **lake in the volcanic hollow**, not the volcano. ❗ The collapse mechanism is stated only to the depth required to **discriminate a crater from a caldera**, and everything beyond that is routed out. 🔁 Topic 02 owns the Deccan Traps as a lithological and stratigraphic unit. 🔁 Environment and Ecology owns the ecology and the microbiology of alkaline lakes.
Trap: that a roughly circular lake in volcanic rock is a volcanic crater lake. ❗ The defusal is the three-way discrimination this lesson builds — **explosive crater**, **collapse caldera**, **impact structure** — in which the Indian type case belongs to the third and sits inside basalt laid down by the first family's parent process, which is exactly why the error is so easy to make.

### 1. The intuition: three ways to end up with a round hole, only two of them volcanic

⚠️ Stand at the rim of a roughly circular depression a few kilometres across, holding water, floored and walled in dark volcanic rock. ⚠️ Three completely different histories could have put you there, and from the rim alone they look similar. ❗ **That similarity is the whole reason this lesson exists**, and it is why the examiner can set a question on it.

⚠️ **History one: something blew out of the ground here.** ⚠️ Gas-charged magma reached the surface, fragmented, and was ejected. ⚠️ The ejected material piled up around the vent, building a cone, and the hole at the top of that cone is the **crater** — the place the material came out of. ❗ Note the logic: **the rim is made of the stuff that used to be in the hole**, so the rim is **constructional** and the hollow is small relative to the edifice it sits on.

⚠️ **History two: the ground here fell in.** ⚠️ A large eruption emptied a magma chamber at depth. The roof over that chamber was then unsupported, and it **subsided into the void**. ❗ The hollow is a **caldera**, and its size is set not by a vent but by **the plan area of the magma chamber that emptied** — which is why calderas are measured in kilometres to tens of kilometres and craters in hundreds of metres. ❗ The rim is not built; it is **what is left of an edifice that has been truncated**, so the rim is **destructional**. 🔁 The mechanics of chamber evacuation and roof failure belong to Topic 03 and are not developed here.

⚠️ **History three: something hit the ground here from outside.** ⚠️ A body arriving at hypervelocity delivered its kinetic energy in microseconds. ⚠️ Rock was vaporised, melted, shocked and excavated; the target rock around the hole was **structurally uplifted and overturned**. ❗ The hollow is an **impact structure**, and its three diagnostics separate it cleanly from the other two: **there is no cone, no vent and no edifice**; the hole is cut into a surface that was **flat before the event**; and the rim carries **inverted stratigraphy and shock-metamorphic features** rather than ejecta from below.

> 🔑 **The one-line discriminator:** ❗ a **crater** is where the material **came out**; a **caldera** is where the ground **went down**; an **impact structure** is where something **came in**.

### 2. The four volcanic lake forms, and what each implies

✅ Leong divides Class 3 into three; the canonical owner adds impact as a **separate row**, which makes four forms a candidate must be able to tell apart.

**(1) Crater lake — explosive hollow at the summit.** ✅ Leong names **Avernus**. ⚠️ **Mechanism:** water collects in the vent-top hollow of a cone, fed by rainfall and sometimes by groundwater. ⚠️ **Geometry:** small, near-circular, steep-sided, often deep for its tiny area, and **perched high** on the edifice. ⚠️ **Hydrology, and this is the interesting part:** a summit crater lake has an **extremely small catchment** — essentially the inner walls of the crater and nothing else — so its budget is dominated by **direct precipitation and evaporation**, with almost no river inflow. ❗ That makes it a near-pure rain-fed closed system, and therefore **chemically distinctive**: it concentrates whatever the volcano exhales, which is why many crater lakes are **acidic** rather than saline. ⚠️ **Change behaviour:** it can be destroyed in a single event by renewed eruption, and it can fail by **breaching its rim**, which is a soft pile of ejecta.

**(2) Caldera lake — collapse hollow.** ✅ Leong names **Crater Lake, Oregon**, with the explicit and valuable correction that it *"in fact occupies a **caldera**"* despite its name, and **Toba**. ⚠️ **Mechanism:** roof subsidence into an evacuated chamber. ⚠️ **Geometry:** ❗ **large — the defining property** — with a broad, relatively flat floor, and frequently a **later cone or resurgent dome sitting on that floor as an island**. ⚠️ That island is itself a diagnostic: nothing in a crater or an impact structure produces a central constructional cone. ⚠️ **Hydrology:** a caldera lake has a **much larger catchment** than a crater lake and can be very deep, giving a **long residence time**. ⚠️ **Change behaviour:** immensely long-lived in the absence of renewed activity, because the floor is rock and the catchment small relative to the volume.

❗ **Leong's Crater Lake sentence deserves to be used, not merely quoted.** ⚠️ It is a book doing in one clause exactly what this edition asks candidates to do: **refusing to let a name determine a classification.** ⚠️ A lake called "Crater Lake" is a caldera lake. 🇮🇳 ❗ The Indian parallel is exact and is developed in §4 — a lake sitting in dark volcanic rock in a region defined by volcanism is **not** volcanic.

**(3) Lava-dammed lake — a flow blocks a valley.** ✅ Leong names the **Sea of Galilee**. ⚠️ **Mechanism:** a lava flow crosses and blocks a pre-existing drainage line; water ponds behind it. ❗ Note that this is **not a volcanic hollow at all** — the basin is an ordinary valley, and the volcanic contribution is **the dam**. ⚠️ **Geometry:** valley-shaped, elongate, with the barrier at the downstream end — in plan it resembles a morainic-dammed or landslide-dammed lake, and the discrimination is entirely in **what the dam is made of**. ⚠️ **Change behaviour:** ❗ a lava dam is **far stronger** than a moraine or landslide dam, because it is solid rock rather than loose debris, so a lava-dammed lake is **much more durable** than the superficially similar forms at Lessons 4 and 7. ⚠️ Its vulnerability is to **incision** — the outlet stream slowly cutting a notch through the flow — and to **seepage** through the flow's columnar joints and lava tubes, which can be substantial.

> ⚠️ **[U-08] A source flag, not a correction.** ✅ Leong attributes the **Sea of Galilee** to lava damming. ⚠️ The Sea of Galilee lies in the **Jordan Rift**, a major structural depression, and the lava-dam attribution is **contested**. ❗ **This edition attributes the claim to the book and flags the dispute; it does not adjudicate it and it does not assert a structural origin either.** ⚠️ The pedagogic value is preserved regardless: the lesson needs a lava-dammed form, and the mechanism is sound even where the type example is disputed.

**(4) Volcanic-subsidence lake — ground settling over a volcanic region.** ✅ Leong names **Myvatn**. ⚠️ **Mechanism:** broad settling of a volcanic terrain — distinct from caldera collapse, which is localised over a chamber — producing a shallow, irregular depression. ⚠️ **Geometry:** shallow, irregular, often with **lava features emerging as islands and pseudo-craters** within it. ⚠️ **Change behaviour:** shallow, so high area-to-volume and vulnerable to infill; but set in young, permeable volcanic rock, so groundwater exchange can be large and the lake may be fed and drained substantially below the surface.

### 3. ❌ Correction and separation box — impact is a separate genetic class

> ❗ **The canonical owner creates a separate origin-table row for Impact, distinct from Volcanic**, and its §3.3 states the point without qualification: ✅ *"**Lonar** is a meteorite-impact lake excavated in Deccan basalt, **not** a volcanic crater."* ✅ The owner's scale-and-map control clause reinforces it by requiring that the *"Lonar impact basin"* retain its *"exact spatial/genetic qualifier"*. ❗ **This is a terminology-control instruction, and it is binding on this edition.**
>
> ⚠️ **Why the confusion is structural rather than careless.** ⚠️ The Indian case sits **inside the Deccan Traps**, a province defined by one of the largest basaltic volcanic events in Earth history. ⚠️ A near-circular depression, a few kilometres wide, with a raised rim, holding water, entirely within basalt, in a province named for volcanism, **looks exactly like a crater lake and is in precisely the place a crater lake would be expected.** ❗ The discrimination therefore cannot be made from setting, from rock type or from plan shape. ❗ It has to be made from **mechanism evidence**: the absence of a cone, vent or edifice; the hollow cut into a pre-existing flat basalt surface; and a rim of **structurally uplifted and overturned target rock**, with shock-metamorphic features in that rock.
>
> ❗ **The examinable form of the point.** ⚠️ Classification is **genetic**, not lithological. ⚠️ "In volcanic rock" describes the **target**, not the **process**. ❗ A lake excavated by an external impactor into basalt is an **impact lake in basalt**, and the basalt is a circumstance of location, not a term in the genesis.

❗ **The limitation, stated rather than skipped.** ⚠️ **No diameter, depth, age, salinity or alkalinity figure for the Indian case appears anywhere in this edition**, because no local book in this evidence base supplies one and no first-party dated source was obtained for it during this generation pass. ⚠️ What is taught is the **classification and the reason for it**, which is what the canonical owner supplies and what the demand family actually tests.

### 4. 🇮🇳 India example — Lonar, in Buldhana, and the trap it generates

🇮🇳 ✅ **Lonar lies in Buldhana district, Maharashtra, within the Deccan basalt.** ✅ The canonical owner classifies it as a **meteorite-impact lake**, in a **separate origin row** from volcanic lakes, and states that it is *"excavated in Deccan basalt, not a volcanic crater."*

🇮🇳 ⚠️ **What makes Lonar valuable to a Mains answer is not the fact but the three-way discrimination it forces**, and that discrimination is reusable on any unfamiliar circular lake.

| Question to ask | Volcanic crater | Volcanic caldera | ❗ Impact structure |
|---|---|---|---|
| Is there a **cone or edifice** around the hollow? | ⚠️ Yes — the hollow sits on it | ⚠️ A **truncated remnant** of one | ❗ **No. None.** The hollow is cut into a pre-existing flat surface |
| What is the **rim made of**? | ⚠️ **Ejecta** piled up — constructional | ⚠️ The **cut-off flank** of a former edifice — destructional | ❗ **Target rock, structurally uplifted and overturned** — inverted stratigraphy |
| What sets the **diameter**? | ⚠️ The **vent** — small | ⚠️ The **plan area of the emptied chamber** — large | ❗ The **impactor's energy** — independent of any volcanic structure |
| Is there a **central cone or dome on the floor**? | ⚠️ No | ⚠️ Often, and it is diagnostic | ❗ In large structures a **central uplift** may exist, but it is **uplifted target rock, not lava** |
| What is the **decisive physical evidence**? | ⚠️ Vent and ejecta stratigraphy | ⚠️ Collapse structure and chamber relationship | ❗ **Shock-metamorphic features in the target rock** |
| **Indian case** | — | — | ✅ **Lonar, Buldhana — impact, in basalt** |

🇮🇳 ❗ **The limitation attached to the Indian case, stated plainly.** ⚠️ Lonar is a **single occurrence**. India has no family of impact lakes and no family of true volcanic crater or caldera lakes, because the Deccan volcanism is **ancient, fissure-fed and flood-basaltic** rather than recent and edifice-building, and the active volcanism in the Indian region is 🔁 Topic 03's material — Barren Island and Narcondam in the Andaman arc, which carry no lakes of this type in the sources available here. ❗ Therefore this lesson **cannot** offer an Indian family and does not pretend to. ⚠️ Its India contribution is a **single, highly examinable discrimination** plus the warning that the Indian example is an **exception within a volcanic province**, which is the hardest kind of case to classify and the most likely to be set.

### 5. The change consequence — what a volcanic origin implies for how the lake will die

❗ [R-01] The genesis lesson closes on change.

| Form | Residence-time profile | Dominant death mode | Distinctive risk |
|---|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ **Crater** | ❗ Tiny catchment, so budget is **rain minus evaporation**; small volume; variable | ⚠️ **Obliteration by renewed eruption**, or **rim breach** through loose ejecta | ❗ Chemically extreme — concentrates volcanic exhalations; frequently **acidic** |
| ⚠️ **Caldera** | ❗ Large volume, moderate catchment → **long residence time** | ⚠️ Extremely slow **infill**; death is effectively geological | ❗ Long residence integrates everything that enters; recovery would take centuries |
| ⚠️ **Lava-dammed** | ⚠️ Valley catchment → moderate throughflow | ⚠️ **Outlet incision through the flow**, plus **seepage** through joints and lava tubes | ❗ A **strong** dam, unlike a moraine or landslide dam — this is the durable member of the dammed family |
| ⚠️ **Volcanic-subsidence** | ⚠️ Shallow, high area-to-volume, large groundwater exchange | ⚠️ **Infill**, accelerated by shallowness | ⚠️ Budget partly invisible, because much of the exchange is subsurface |
| ❗ **Impact** | ⚠️ Closed or near-closed, small catchment confined to the rim | ❗ **Hydrological** — evaporation-dominated, so **salinisation and alkalinisation**, exactly as Lesson 2 predicts for a closed basin | ❗ Its water chemistry is a **budget outcome**, not an impact outcome |

❗ **The last row is the point where this lesson rejoins the spine of the edition and it is worth stating carefully.** ⚠️ An impact structure's **rim is continuous and raised**, so the hollow it encloses is typically a **closed basin with a catchment no larger than the structure itself**. ⚠️ Apply Lesson 2 and the consequence is immediate: water leaves only by evaporation and seepage, dissolved solids have no exit, and the lake becomes **saline and, where the target rock supplies the relevant ions, strongly alkaline**. ❗ **The chemistry is a consequence of the budget, not of the impact.** ⚠️ The impact made the hollow and sealed the rim; everything thereafter is Lesson 2's mass balance running for as long as the structure has existed. ❗ That is the analytically strongest sentence available on this topic and it should be the closing line of any answer that mentions the Indian case.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | What it explains | What it does not explain |
|---|---|---|
| **Crater against caldera by mechanism** | Why diameter differs by orders of magnitude; why one rim is built and the other truncated; why a central cone can sit on a caldera floor | The eruption dynamics that produce either — 🔁 Topic 03's |
| **Impact as a separate class** | Why a round lake in basalt need not be volcanic; why classification is genetic, not lithological | The impactor, its age or its energy, none of which is in this evidence base |
| **The dam-material argument** | Why a lava-dammed lake outlives a morainic-dammed or landslide-dammed one of identical plan | Whether any particular valley dam is lava, moraine or landslide, which is a field question |

**Strongest objection.** ⚠️ Volcanic lakes are a tiny fraction of the world's lakes and India has effectively none of the true volcanic types. Devoting a full lesson to a class with one Indian exception, and that exception not even volcanic, is disproportionate.

**Reply.** ❗ Three reasons, the third operative. ⚠️ **First**, the canonical owner gives volcanic and impact **two separate origin-table rows** and a dedicated §3.3, which makes the class owned content rather than enrichment. ⚠️ **Second**, this is **not** a volcanic-lake gazetteer but a **discrimination lesson**, and the discrimination — constructional against destructional rims, hollow size against edifice size, internal against external energy source — is a transferable habit that reappears whenever a candidate meets a circular water body. ❗ **Third**, the 2018 owned demand requires identifying an **artificial** lake among named Indian water bodies, which is a demand to distinguish genesis **from appearance**. ⚠️ Lonar is the purest training case for exactly that skill, because every surface cue points one way and the correct classification goes the other.

⚠️ **Residual.** This edition carries **no quantitative information at all** about the Indian case — no diameter, depth, age, salinity, alkalinity or catchment area — because none is available in its evidence base and none was obtained from a first-party dated source during this generation pass. ❗ **No figure for Lonar is stated anywhere in this file.** ⚠️ Equally, **[U-08]** the lava-dam attribution for the type example of form (3) is **contested and is left unresolved**, with the mechanism taught and the example attributed.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** General Studies Paper I, the mapped change clause. ⚠️ This lesson's change contribution is unusual and worth naming: it shows that **a lake's chemistry can be a budget outcome of its rim geometry**, which is the bridge from genesis to Lesson 2 and on to Lessons 9 and 11.
- **Verified linkage:** ⚠️ No owned demand is answered by this lesson alone. ❗ It builds the **genesis-against-appearance discrimination** behind **2018, Prelims GS-I, Q77** — identifying an artificial lake among named Indian water bodies, ❗ **key unavailable locally, no answer recorded or inferred** — and it supplies the classification the canonical owner's control clause requires for the Indian impact basin.
- **Traps:** calling the Indian case a volcanic crater lake; inferring genesis from lithology; assuming a lake named "Crater Lake" occupies a crater; treating size as the mechanism rather than as its symptom; assuming all dammed lakes are equally fragile when a lava dam is rock; asserting a structural origin for the Sea of Galilee when the source attributes it to lava and the question is contested.
- **Answer use:** ⚠️ For any question touching circular or volcanic-looking lakes, lead with the **energy-source triad**. ❗ *"Three mechanisms make a round hollow: material blown out, forming a crater on the edifice it built; a roof dropped into an emptied chamber, forming a caldera far wider than any vent; and excavation by an external impactor, which leaves no cone and a rim of overturned target rock. Classification is genetic, so the rock the hollow is cut into is not part of the answer."* ⚠️ About **sixty words**, and it earns the discrimination mark outright.

Revision notes:

- 🔑 **The one-line triad:** a **crater** is where material **came out**; a **caldera** is where the ground **went down**; an **impact structure** is where something **came in**.
- ⚠️ **Crater:** small hollow at the summit of the cone the eruption **built**; rim is **constructional ejecta**; catchment is only the inner walls, so the budget is **rain minus evaporation**; frequently **acidic**. ✅ Leong: **Avernus**.
- ⚠️ **Caldera:** hollow formed by **roof subsidence into an emptied magma chamber**; ❗ **diameter is set by the chamber's plan area**, so it is far larger than any vent; rim is a **truncated remnant**; a **later cone or resurgent dome on the floor** is diagnostic. ✅ Leong: **Toba**, and **Crater Lake, Oregon**, which *"in fact occupies a caldera"*.
- ❗ **Size discriminates in practice; mechanism discriminates in principle.** Say the mechanism, use the size as evidence.
- ⚠️ **Lava-dammed:** the basin is an **ordinary valley**; the volcanic contribution is **the dam**. ❗ A lava dam is **rock**, so it is **far more durable** than a moraine or landslide dam of the same plan. ⚠️ Dies by **outlet incision** and **seepage through columnar joints and lava tubes**. ✅ Leong: **Sea of Galilee** — ❗ **[U-08] attribution contested, flagged, not adjudicated.**
- ⚠️ **Volcanic-subsidence:** broad settling of a volcanic terrain, **shallow and irregular**, with large **subsurface** exchange. ✅ Leong: **Myvatn**.
- ❗ **Impact is a SEPARATE canonical origin row**, not a sub-type of volcanic. ✅ *"Lonar is a meteorite-impact lake excavated in Deccan basalt, not a volcanic crater."*
- ❗ **The three impact diagnostics:** **no cone, no vent, no edifice**; hollow cut into a **pre-existing flat surface**; rim of **structurally uplifted and overturned target rock** with **inverted stratigraphy** and shock features.
- ❗ **Classification is genetic, not lithological.** *"In volcanic rock"* describes the **target**, not the **process**.
- 🇮🇳 ✅ **Lonar, Buldhana district, Maharashtra**, within the **Deccan basalt** — ❗ the hardest classification case in Indian physical geography because **every surface cue points the wrong way**.
- ❗ **The closing analytical line:** an impact rim is **continuous and raised**, so the basin is **closed** with a catchment no larger than the structure; therefore by Lesson 2 it **salinises and alkalinises**. ❗ **The chemistry is a budget outcome, not an impact outcome.**
- ⚠️ **India has no family of true volcanic crater or caldera lakes**, because Deccan volcanism was **ancient, fissure-fed and flood-basaltic**, not recent and edifice-building. ❗ Name the absence rather than inventing an example.
- ❗ **No diameter, depth, age or chemistry figure for Lonar appears in this edition**, because none is available to it.
- 🔁 **Routed out:** eruption mechanics, magma-chamber behaviour, caldera-collapse dynamics and volcanic hazard, all to **Topic 03 (released, 16 lessons)**; the Deccan Traps as a stratigraphic unit to **Topic 02**; alkaline-lake ecology and microbiology to **Environment and Ecology**.

### Concept check

**Question:** You are given two near-circular lakes. Lake X is 400 metres across, sits in a hollow at the top of a 900-metre conical hill built of layered ash and lapilli, and its water is strongly acidic. Lake Y is 9 kilometres across, has a broad flat floor, is ringed by steep cliffs that expose layered lavas truncated at the rim, and carries a small wooded cone rising as an island from its floor. A third lake, Z, is roughly circular, lies entirely within an extensive basalt plateau, has a raised rim of basalt whose layering is **inverted** relative to the surrounding plateau, shows no cone or vent anywhere, and its water is strongly saline and alkaline. Classify all three, state the diagnostic that settles each, and explain Z's water chemistry.

**Model answer:** **Lake X is a volcanic crater lake.** The hollow sits at the **summit of a cone** built of **layered ash and lapilli** — fragmental material ejected from the vent — so the edifice is **constructional** and is made of what used to occupy the hollow. Four hundred metres against a 900-metre edifice is the ratio a vent produces; a collapse structure would be comparable to or larger than its surviving edifice. The acidity is corroborating: a summit crater lake's catchment is only its own inner walls, so its budget is direct precipitation against evaporation and it concentrates what the volcano exhales. **Lake Y is a caldera lake.** The decisive evidence is the **truncation**: cliffs exposing layered lavas **cut off** at the rim mean the rim is the sawn-off flank of a former edifice, which is destructional. Nine kilometres is far wider than any vent and reflects the **plan area of the subsided magma chamber**; the flat floor is the subsided block; and the wooded cone rising as an island is a **later constructional edifice built on that floor** — a feature neither a crater nor an impact structure produces. Leong makes the naming point himself: Crater Lake in Oregon *"in fact occupies a caldera"*. **Lake Z is an impact structure**, proved by the inverted rim. There is **no cone, no vent and no edifice**; the hollow is cut into a **pre-existing flat basalt surface**; and the rim's layering is **inverted**, the signature of target rock structurally uplifted and overturned. The basalt is the **target**, not the process. **Z's chemistry is a budget outcome:** a continuous raised rim makes the basin **closed** with a catchment no larger than itself, so solutes accumulate and, with basalt supplying sodium and carbonate while calcium precipitates early, the water turns strongly alkaline.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not use the rock a hollow is cut into as evidence of the process that cut it. Genetic classification asks **what made the depression**, and an external impactor can excavate a basin in basalt, limestone, sandstone or granite indifferently. The lithology tells you about the **target**; the rim structure, the presence or absence of an edifice, and the shock evidence tell you about the **mechanism**.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "The classification of a lake basin is a statement about process, not about the rock in which it lies." Discuss with reference to volcanic and impact lakes. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** Genetic classification names the process that made the hollow, so lithology is circumstantial. Three processes make round basins. An explosive eruption blows material out, building a cone whose summit hollow is a crater, small relative to the edifice, with a constructional ejecta rim — Leong's Avernus. A large eruption empties a magma chamber whose roof subsides, giving a caldera whose diameter reflects the chamber's plan area, with a truncated rim and often a later cone on its floor; Leong notes that Crater Lake in Oregon in fact occupies a caldera, so a name settles nothing. An external impactor excavates a basin with no cone, no vent and a rim of overturned target rock. Lonar, in Buldhana, lies within Deccan basalt yet is an impact lake, because the basalt is the target rather than the agent. Its saline, alkaline water is a closed-basin budget outcome.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** **4** for crater and caldera discriminated **by mechanism** — material ejected against roof subsided — rather than by size alone; **3** for the diameter-and-rim evidence deployed, including the constructional-against-destructional rim distinction; **3** for the Indian classification caveat handled correctly, naming impact as a separate genetic class and stating that lithology describes the target. Deduct 2 for classifying the Indian case as volcanic; deduct 1 for using size as the mechanism rather than as its symptom.

          |  and COLLAPSE of cave roofs beneath
          v
   [5] HOLLOWS COALESCE INTO A LARGE, FLAT-FLOORED CLOSED BASIN
