### 1. The intuition: the only lake class whose cause has intentions

⚠️ Five classes of lake are made by processes that are indifferent to the outcome. ⚠️ A fault does not intend a lake; it is relieving stress. ⚠️ Ice does not intend a tarn. ❗ **Class 6 is different: a reservoir exists because somebody decided it should, for a purpose, at a chosen place, at a chosen size.**

⚠️ Three consequences follow, and all three are examinable.

❗ **One: artificial lakes are sited where they are useful, not where hollows happen to be.** ⚠️ A natural lake tells you about the **process history of its region**; a reservoir tells you about **where people needed water, power or flood control**, and about the engineering constraint that a dam needs a narrow valley with sound abutments. ❗ **The distribution of reservoirs is therefore a map of demand and of valley geometry, not of geomorphic history** — which is itself a finding worth stating.

❗ **Two: they are operated, which means their water level is a policy variable.** ⚠️ A natural lake's level is set by its budget. ⚠️ A reservoir's level is set by **release decisions** — drawn down before the monsoon to create flood cushion, filled during it, drawn down through the dry season for irrigation and power. ❗ That makes the `A` term of Lesson 1's ledger **dominant and discretionary**, which is a condition no natural lake experiences.

❗ **Three, and most importantly for this edition: they have the same life cycle as a natural lake, compressed.** ⚠️ [A7] A reservoir fills, stratifies, receives sediment, loses storage, and is eventually abandoned — exactly the sequence a natural lake undergoes, but telescoped from millennia into decades, because the basin is shallow relative to its catchment and the catchment is usually large. ❗ **India supplies the measured proof of that compression, and it is worked in §5 and quantified at Lesson 12.**

### 2. The human sub-types, from Leong's own list

✅ Leong, printed 69, divides Class 6 into human and biological and gives four human forms plus one biological one.

**(6a) Reservoirs.** ✅ Leong gives **Lake Mead above the Hoover Dam** with **Figure 72**. ⚠️ A dam is built across a valley; the valley floods upstream of it. ❗ The impounded water body takes the **plan of the drowned valley**, which is the single most reliable identification of a reservoir on a map and is developed in §4.

**(6b) Tanks.** ⚠️ Leong does not use the Indian term, but ✅ Khullar, printed folio **594**, defines it exactly: a tank is ***"a bund of earth or stone thrown across a stream"***, partly dug out. ❗ This is a reservoir in miniature, and India's tradition of it is the largest in the world. ⚠️ Khullar gives the scale and the trend, both with their vintages attached: ✅ roughly **5 lakh big and 50 lakh small tanks**, irrigating about **1.64 million hectares**; and the tank share of India's total irrigated area falling from ✅ **14 per cent in 1960-61 to about 3.0 per cent in 2009-10**. ✅ By state: **Andhra Pradesh with Telangana, 727 thousand hectares, about 28.8 per cent of India's tank-irrigated area**; **Tamil Nadu, 589 thousand hectares, about 23 per cent, with roughly 24,000 tanks**. ✅ And the source context, from Khullar's Figure 16.2, *"Net area under irrigation by sources 2009-10"*, after the *Statistical Year Book India 2013*, p.12.3: **tube wells and other wells 62 per cent, canals 26 per cent, tanks 3 per cent, other 9 per cent**.

> ❗ **[N-16] Every figure in that paragraph carries its vintage: the tank series is 1960-61 to 2009-10, and Figure 16.2 is 2009-10 after the *Statistical Year Book India 2013*.** ❗ **None of them is offered as current.**
> ⚠️ **[U-06] A source flag, not a correction.** ✅ *"5 lakh big and 50 lakh small tanks"* is quoted **with the book attributed**. ❗ Tank censuses vary enormously by **definition and size threshold**, exactly as lake counts do, and no threshold is stated on the page. ❗ **Flagged, not adjudicated, and no alternative count is asserted.**
> ⚠️ **[U-07] An ambiguity in the source, handled by omission.** ✅ Khullar's folio 595 says *"16 per cent of the total irrigated area of **the state**"* immediately after naming **two** states, so the antecedent is ambiguous. ❗ **That sentence is omitted from this edition entirely.** ❗ Only the unambiguous figures — 727 thousand hectares and 28.8 per cent; 589 thousand hectares and 23 per cent — are quoted.

**(6c) Mining lakes.** ✅ Leong names **tin working in West Malaysia**. ⚠️ Extraction leaves a void; the void fills with groundwater and rainfall. ❗ Note the genetic oddity: the **mechanism of excavation is human, but the filling is entirely natural**, and the resulting water body's budget obeys Lesson 1 without modification. ⚠️ In India the equivalents are abandoned quarries, opencast mine voids and brick-clay pits, which have the same signature — **steep, often vertical margins, a geometric plan, and a depth out of all proportion to their tiny area**.

**(6d) Ornamental and fishing lakes.** ✅ Leong names **Lake Gardens Kuala Lumpur** and the **Taiping Lakes**, plus lakes made for fish breeding. ⚠️ Small, shallow, usually in urban parkland, with an engineered margin. ❗ Their analytical value is disproportionate to their size, because they are the **urban lakes whose catchments are sealed** — and that is the setting in which cultural eutrophication is most intense, developed at Lesson 10.

**(6e) Biological — beaver dams.** ✅ Leong names **Beaver Lake in Yellowstone**. ⚠️ An organism other than a human builds a barrier across a stream. ❗ This is the only genetic class in the whole taxonomy created by a **non-human animal**, and it is worth one line because it makes the classification honest: the operative distinction in Class 6 is not *"made by people"* but **"made by an organism acting deliberately on the drainage."** ⚠️ India has no beaver; the Indian analogues are **not** biological dams but the **phumdis of Loktak**, which are floating masses of vegetation and soil that modify a lake from within rather than damming it — a distinct phenomenon treated at Lesson 15.

### 3. ❌ Correction box — [N-01] the Hirakud Dam is in Odisha, not Madhya Pradesh

> ❌ **Leong, printed folio 70 (local scan PDF p.73), writes of *"the Hirakud Dam (**Madhya Pradesh**) on the Mahanadi in India"*. That is factually wrong.**
> ✅ **The dam is in Odisha, and three independent sources in this edition's evidence base say so.**
> ✅ **One — D.R. Khullar, printed folio 611:** *"3. THE HIRAKUD DAM PROJECT — It is an ambitious project of **Odisha** ... 61 m high and 4,801 m long dam on the **Mahanadi at Hirakud about 14 km upstream of Sambalpur** ... one of the longest dams in the world ... gross storage **8,100 million cubic metres** over an area of **630 sq km"***, with further dams at **Tikrapara** and **Naraj**, irrigation to **1 million hectares** in **Sambalpur, Bolangir, Puri and Cuttack**, and **3.5 lakh kW**. ❗ [N-16] All book-era Khullar figures, folio-attributed.
> ✅ **Two — Majid Husain, *Geography of India*, printed 8.19, Table 8.9**, read off the render: *"11. **Orissa** — Hirakud (Mahanadi), Balimela"*. ❗ [N-13] **Orissa → Odisha** in current naming.
> ✅ **Three — the repository map bank's own rows for Hirakud record Odisha.**
> ❗ **Why this correction is not pedantry.** ⚠️ 🔁 **2022, Prelims Question 70**, on major reservoirs and their states, is routed to `Geography/basic/36`. ⚠️ A candidate carrying Leong's error carries a **specific, testable falsehood about exactly the kind of pairing that demand uses** — and the error would propagate into any Topic 09 answer that names Hirakud as an artificial-lake example. ❗ **The error is named openly here and the correction is asserted again at Lesson 12.**
> ❗ **[N-11] A second, softer correction on the same page family.** ✅ Majid, printed 8.19, calls Bhakra *"the **highest straightway gravity dam in the world** ... 518 m long and 226 m high"*. ❗ **The dimensions are retained with the book attributed; the world ranking is obsolete and is dropped.** ⚠️ **[U-05]** The same span dates the Damodar Valley Corporation to *"February 18, 1948"* where the commonly cited date is 7 July 1948; ❗ **the book date is presented attributed, the discrepancy is flagged, and it is not adjudicated** — the Corporation is marginal to Topic 09 in any case.
> ❗ **[N-13] Three further normalisations from Leong's printed folio 70, which is the India-bearing page of his chapter.** ✅ *"The **Okhla** Reservoir supplies **Delhi** and the **Vetarna**, **Vihar** and **Tulsi** lakes supply **Bombay**"* → ❗ **Vaitarna**, and **Mumbai**. ⚠️ ✅ The same page names the **Lloyd Barrage on the Indus at Sukkur** — now in Pakistan — and ✅ **the Bhakra and Nangal Dams on the Sutlej** as flow-regulation examples. ⚠️ ✅ It also gives **the Red River Valley of Canada as the bed of former Lake Agassiz**, which is the world's best illustration of the point made at Lesson 3 about the Karewas: **a dead lake's floor is farmland**.

### 4. [A7] and the discrimination method — telling an artificial lake from a natural one

❗ **This is the capability behind the 2018 owned demand, which asks for the identification of an artificial lake among named Indian water bodies.** ❗ **The official key for that demand is unavailable locally. No option, no candidate list, no elimination and no answer appears here or anywhere in this edition.** ❗ What is taught is the **method**, which is what transfers to names the examiner has not yet used.

```text
   THE NATURAL-AGAINST-ARTIFICIAL DISCRIMINATION, SIX TESTS
   (none of these requires knowing the water body's name)

   TEST 1  PLAN SHAPE
     natural    : controlled by the basin-forming process --
                  crescent (ox-bow), circular (crater/impact/kettle),
                  elongate-straight (rift), elongate-sinuous-with-
                  the-valley (ribbon), coast-parallel (lagoon)
     ARTIFICIAL : ** DENDRITIC ** -- the drowned valley and ALL its
                  tributary valleys, giving a branching, many-armed
                  plan that NO natural process produces

   TEST 2  THE DOWNSTREAM END
     natural    : a shoreline, a sill, a bar, a plug -- continuous
                  with the surrounding land
     ARTIFICIAL : ** A STRAIGHT LINE ACROSS THE VALLEY ** -- the dam.
                  Straightness at that scale is never natural.

   TEST 3  THE MARGIN
     natural    : a littoral shelf, a beach, reed fringe, delta --
                  the lake has had time to build its own shore
     ARTIFICIAL : ** DROWNED HILLSLOPES ** running straight into the
                  water with NO shelf, because the slope is a
                  valley side, not a shore. Often a bare DRAWDOWN
                  ZONE above the water line.

   TEST 4  RELATION TO THE RIVER
     natural    : the lake is ON the river (through-flowing), or
                  BESIDE it (ox-bow), or has no river at all (closed)
     ARTIFICIAL : the lake IS the river, backed up. Upstream it
                  TAPERS into the live channel with no boundary.

   TEST 5  LEVEL BEHAVIOUR
     natural    : level follows the BUDGET -- seasonal, smooth
     ARTIFICIAL : level follows OPERATING RULES -- drawn down before
                  the monsoon, filled during it, drawn down through
                  the dry season. Often a LARGE, ABRUPT range with
                  a conspicuous bath-tub ring.

   TEST 6  SETTING AND PURPOSE
     natural    : sited where the PROCESS put it
     ARTIFICIAL : sited where a NARROW VALLEY WITH SOUND ABUTMENTS
                  meets a DEMAND for water, power or flood control

   ===> THE TANK IS THE HARD CASE, AND MUST BE SAID SO.
        A small tank fails tests 1 and 3 -- it can look like a
        natural pond. It passes tests 2 and 6 decisively: there is
        a BUND, and it is sited to command a slope. The giveaway
        is the ** STRAIGHT EARTH OR STONE BUND ACROSS THE LOWER
        SIDE ONLY **, with the other three sides following the
        natural ground.
```

❗ **[A7] The deeper point, which is the lesson's analytical centre: the artificial lake has the same life cycle as the natural one, compressed.** ⚠️ Once built, a reservoir obeys Lesson 1's ledger exactly. ⚠️ It fills. ⚠️ Being deep behind the dam and shallow upstream, it **stratifies** in the deep section, as Lesson 9 will describe. ⚠️ Its velocity drops to near zero at the dam, so it **traps essentially all the sediment its river delivers** — which is Lesson 12's trap-efficiency argument. ⚠️ Sediment accumulates from the tail downstream, consuming first the **dead storage** below the lowest outlet and then the **live storage** above it. ❗ And eventually the storage is gone and the structure is a weir on a sediment plain. ⚠️ **That is precisely Leong's "double process of draining and silting up", running at engineering speed instead of geological speed.** ❗ **India supplies the measured series that proves it, and the arithmetic is shown at Lesson 12.**

### 5. 🇮🇳 India example — the tank tradition, and the two great reservoirs

🇮🇳 ❗ **India's tank tradition is the best demonstration anywhere in this edition that an artificial lake dies by the same two mechanisms as a natural one**, because Khullar lists the failure modes explicitly and every one of them maps onto a term in Lesson 1's ledger.

✅ **Khullar, printed folio 594-595 — why tanks exist, in four causes.** ✅ **Undulating relief**, which supplies natural catchments and sites; ✅ **hard, impermeable rock**, which prevents the percolation that would otherwise make wells easy and makes surface storage the rational choice; ✅ **seasonal torrential streams**, which deliver water in bursts that must be stored or lost; ✅ **scattered fields**, which suit small decentralised storage rather than a canal network. ❗ **All four are physical-geographical**, which is why the tank belt coincides with the hard-rock Peninsula and not with the alluvial north.

✅ **Khullar's demerits of tanks — read them as a budget failure list.**

| ✅ Khullar's stated demerit | ⚠️ The ledger term or mechanism it names |
|---|---|
| ✅ *"Tanks dry up when most needed"* | ❗ `Qi` is **seasonal and torrential**; storage is small; the drought year is exactly the year inflow fails |
| ✅ *"The bed silts and needs regular desilting"* | ❗ **Sedimentary death**, Lesson 12 — the basin fills |
| ✅ *"Evaporation is high from a shallow spread"* | ❗ **[A4] The shallow-basin multiplier** — high area-to-volume means large `E` per unit stored |
| ✅ *"Tanks occupy cultivable land"* | ⚠️ An opportunity cost, not a hydrological term — the pressure that drives the next row |
| ✅ *"Dry beds get reclaimed for agriculture"* | ❗ **Conversion** — the terminal step, and the direct Indian analogue of the 2021 Mains demand on reclaiming water bodies, which is 🔁 Environment-owned |
| ✅ *"Lifting is costly"* | ⚠️ Economic, not hydrological — routed to the irrigation and economy owners |

❗ **The third row is the one to carry into an answer.** ⚠️ A tank is shallow by construction, so its area-to-volume ratio is high, so a large fraction of its stored water evaporates — and the evaporation is **greatest in the hot, dry months when the water is most needed**. ❗ **A tank therefore fails hardest in exactly the conditions it was built for**, which is the clearest Indian illustration of [A4] in the whole edition. ⚠️ The fall from **14 per cent of irrigated area in 1960-61 to about 3.0 per cent in 2009-10** is the measurable consequence of that structural weakness combined with the rise of tube wells to **62 per cent** by 2009-10. ❗ **All figures book-era Khullar, vintages attached.**

🇮🇳 ✅ **Hirakud, Odisha — the reservoir as a landform.** ✅ Khullar, folio 611: a **61 metre high, 4,801 metre long** dam on the **Mahanadi at Hirakud, about 14 kilometres upstream of Sambalpur**, *"one of the longest dams in the world"*, with **gross storage 8,100 million cubic metres over an area of 630 square kilometres**, further dams at **Tikrapara** and **Naraj**, irrigation to **1 million hectares** in **Sambalpur, Bolangir, Puri and Cuttack**, and **3.5 lakh kW**. ❗ **Book-era, folio-attributed; [N-01] the state is Odisha, correcting Leong.** ⚠️ Note the **ratio** that makes it a lake rather than a pond: 8,100 million cubic metres over 630 square kilometres gives a **mean depth of about 12.9 metres** — shallow for its area, which by [A3] and [A4] predicts both high evaporative exposure and rapid proportional storage loss to sediment.

🇮🇳 ✅ **Bhakra and Gobind Sagar — the reservoir as a measured life cycle.** ✅ Khullar, folio 610, Figure 17.2: the reservoir is **Gobind Sagar**; the main Bhakra canal is **174 kilometres**; gross command is about **27 lakh hectares** and irrigation about **15 lakh hectares**. ✅ And the series that this edition treats as its most valuable single Indian datum: ***"capacity of the reservoir was 6.03 million acre feet in 1963 which was reduced to 5.5 million acre feet in 1988, thereby causing a reduction of over half a million acre feet in a short span of only 25 years."*** ❗ [N-18] The arithmetic, shown rather than asserted: **6.03 − 5.5 = 0.53 million acre feet**, which is what *"over half a million acre feet"* means. ❗ **The full trap-efficiency and dead-storage treatment is Lesson 12's; it is named here only to show that the artificial lake's compressed life cycle is measurable in India.**

🇮🇳 ✅ **Aliyar — an independent local-book occurrence of a name inside an owned demand.** ✅ Majid, *Geography of India*, printed **8.19**, Table 8.9, read off the render: *"14. Tamil Nadu — Mettur, Periyar, **Aliayar**, Kodayar, Moyar, Suruliyar, Papnasam"* — ❗ [N-14] corrected to **Aliyar**. ⚠️ ✅ **2019, Prelims General Studies Paper I, Question 42**, routed by exact filename to `Geography/basic/09_Lakes.md`, asks for **the common feature shared by Aliyar, Isapur and Kangsabati**. ❗ **The official key is unavailable locally. No option, no feature assertion that resolves the demand, no elimination and no answer appears here or anywhere in this edition.** ⚠️ What this lesson supplies is the **inference method**: three unfamiliar names, each of which — on the evidence of where such names appear in Indian sources — sits in the **engineered-water-body register** rather than the natural-lake register. ❗ A candidate who has internalised the six discrimination tests of §4 can reason about what class a name belongs to **from its setting and its company**, which is the capability the demand tests and the only capability that transfers.

### 7. The change consequence — the taxonomy closes on a single prediction

❗ [R-01] This is the last genesis lesson, so its change consequence closes the whole genesis arc.

| Class | Typical depth | Typical area-to-volume | Dominant death mode | Human timescale? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ **1 Earth movement** | ❗ Kilometres | ❗ Very low | ⚠️ Neither, on human timescales — but **long residence accumulates contaminants** | ❌ No |
| ⚠️ **2 Glaciation** | ⚠️ Hundreds of metres | ⚠️ Low to moderate | ⚠️ Infill; ❗ **except the morainic dam, which fails suddenly** | ⚠️ Only the dammed form |
| ⚠️ **3 Volcanic / impact** | ⚠️ Hundreds of metres | ⚠️ Low | ⚠️ Very slow infill; ❗ **impact basins salinise** because closed | ❌ No |
| ⚠️ **4 Erosion** | ⚠️ Tens of metres | ❗ High | ⚠️ Karst **leaks**; playa **salinises** | ⚠️ Seasonally, yes |
| ⚠️ **5 Deposition** | ⚠️ Metres | ❗ Very high | ❗ **Infill, barrier failure, inlet closure — all fast** | ✅ **Yes** |
| ❗ **6 Human / biological** | ⚠️ Tens of metres, shallow for area | ❗ High | ❗ **[A7] Siltation and storage loss — the same cycle, compressed** | ✅ **Yes, and measurably** |

❗ **The single prediction the whole taxonomy delivers:** ⚠️ **the classes that die within a human lifetime are 5 and 6 — the two that make no hollow at all.** ⚠️ Classes 1 to 4 excavate or drop a basin, and their lakes outlast institutions. ⚠️ Classes 5 and 6 place a **barrier across a flow** and detain water in a shallow dish that was never a hollow. ❗ **Those are precisely the lakes that people live beside, farm around, fish in, drink from and lose** — and precisely the lakes the mapped syllabus clause is about. ❗ **Genesis is therefore not a preliminary to the change argument; it is the predictor of it.**

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | What it explains | What it does not explain |
|---|---|---|
| **The six discrimination tests** | How to classify a water body you have never heard of, from plan, outlet, margin and valley relationship | Its name, its state, its capacity — none of which is derivable |
| **[A7] The compressed life cycle** | Why reservoir siltation is a lake-death problem rather than an engineering nuisance | The sediment yield of any catchment, which is Topic 05's and Topic 04's |
| **Khullar's tank demerits** | Why the tank share collapsed; why shallow storage fails in drought | Irrigation policy or the economics of the shift to tube wells |

**Strongest objection.** ⚠️ Reservoirs are engineering structures, and treating them as a genetic class of lake dignifies a classification convenience. The hydrology of a reservoir is operated, not natural, so it is not a geomorphic object at all.

**Reply.** ❗ Three answers, the third decisive. ⚠️ **First**, the canonical owner gives human action its own origin-table row, so the class is owned content. ⚠️ **Second**, the objection has the causality backwards: a reservoir is **operated at its outlet**, but its stratification, sediment trapping, evaporative loss, residence time and progressive shallowing are **pure physical geography and are not controlled by anybody** — the operator sets releases, not the catchment's sediment yield or the evaporation over 630 square kilometres. ❗ **Third**, the 2018 owned demand asks candidates to **identify an artificial lake among named Indian water bodies**, so the examiner has already ruled that the natural-against-artificial distinction is owned Geography content. ⚠️ What is **not** taken is the **matching list**, routed to `Geography/basic/36`, and irrigation policy and canal engineering, routed to the economy and water-resources owners.

⚠️ **Residual.** ❗ **No current reservoir capacity, current storage figure or current tank count appears anywhere in this edition**, because no first-party dated source for any of them was obtained during this generation pass. ⚠️ **[U-06]** the tank counts are threshold-dependent and are flagged rather than reconciled; ⚠️ **[U-07]** the ambiguous state-share sentence is **omitted entirely**; ⚠️ **[U-05]** the Damodar Valley Corporation date discrepancy is **flagged and not adjudicated**.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** General Studies Paper I, the mapped change clause. ❗ This lesson supplies the clause's most tractable case: a **measured** storage loss over a stated 25-year interval, in a named Indian reservoir, from a book that states the figures and the years.
- **Verified linkage:** ✅ **2018, Prelims GS-I, Q77** — identifying an artificial lake among named Indian water bodies — routed to `Geography/basic/09_Lakes.md`. ❗ **Key unavailable locally; no option, candidate list, elimination or answer recorded or inferred.** ⚠️ The six discrimination tests of §4 build exactly that capability. ✅ **2019, Prelims GS-I, Q42** — the common feature of **Aliyar, Isapur and Kangsabati** — same owner. ❗ **Key unavailable locally; no feature assertion that resolves the demand appears.** ⚠️ Majid's printed 8.19 independently places **Aliyar** in the Tamil Nadu hydro row, which is demand context only. 🔁 **2022, Prelims Q70**, reservoirs and their states, is routed to `Geography/basic/36` and is **not** taught here.
- **Traps:** carrying Leong's Hirakud-in-Madhya-Pradesh error; quoting Bhakra as the world's highest gravity dam; quoting tank counts or shares without their vintage; quoting Khullar's ambiguous state-share sentence; classifying a water body by its water rather than by its basin; assuming every large Indian water body with a dam is "a lake" in the natural sense, or that every natural-looking one is natural.
- **Answer use:** ⚠️ For any natural-against-artificial question, lead with the **plan-and-outlet pair**, because it is the fastest discriminator and the most defensible. ❗ *"A reservoir drowns a valley, so its plan is dendritic — the main valley plus every tributary — and its downstream end is a straight line across the valley. No natural process produces either. A natural lake's plan records the process that made its hollow: crescent for a cut-off, circular for a crater or kettle, straight and elongate for a rift."* ⚠️ About **sixty words**, and it earns the discrimination mark without naming a single water body.

Revision notes:

- ❗ **Class 6 is the only class whose cause has intentions.** Reservoirs are sited where a **narrow valley with sound abutments** meets a **demand**, so their distribution maps demand and valley geometry, not geomorphic history. ❗ **A reservoir's level is a policy variable**, not a budget outcome — the `A` term of the ledger is **dominant and discretionary**.
- ✅ **Leong's Class 6:** reservoirs (**Lake Mead above the Hoover Dam**, Figure 72) · **beaver dams** (**Beaver Lake, Yellowstone**) · **ornamental lakes** (**Lake Gardens Kuala Lumpur**, the **Taiping Lakes**) · **mining lakes** (tin working, West Malaysia) · fishing lakes. ✅ **Khullar's tank definition, folio 594:** *"a bund of earth or stone thrown across a stream"*, partly dug out.
- ✅ **Khullar's tank figures, all book-era:** ~**5 lakh big and 50 lakh small** tanks · ~**1.64 million hectares** irrigated · share falling **14 % (1960-61) → ~3.0 % (2009-10)** · **AP with Telangana 727 thousand ha ≈ 28.8 %** · **Tamil Nadu 589 thousand ha ≈ 23 %, ~24,000 tanks** · Figure 16.2 (2009-10, after *Statistical Year Book India 2013*): **wells and tube wells 62 %, canals 26 %, tanks 3 %, other 9 %**.
- ❗ **[U-06] flag:** tank counts are **threshold-dependent**; no threshold is stated on the page. ❗ **[U-07]:** the *"16 per cent ... of the state"* sentence has an **ambiguous antecedent after two states are named** and is **omitted entirely**.
- ✅ **Khullar's four physical causes of tanks:** undulating relief · hard impermeable rock · seasonal torrential streams · scattered fields. ❗ All four are **physical-geographical**, which is why the tank belt is the **hard-rock Peninsula**.
- ❗ **Khullar's tank demerits read as a budget failure list**, and the decisive one is **high evaporation from a shallow spread** — ❗ **[A4]: the tank fails hardest in exactly the drought it was built for.**
- ❌ **[N-01] Correction:** ✅ Leong's *"Hirakud Dam (Madhya Pradesh)"* → ❗ **Odisha**, on three independent sources: ✅ Khullar folio 611 *"an ambitious project of Odisha ... on the Mahanadi at Hirakud about 14 km upstream of Sambalpur"*; ✅ Majid printed 8.19 Table 8.9 *"11. Orissa — Hirakud (Mahanadi), Balimela"*; and the repository map bank.
- ✅ **Hirakud, book-era Khullar folio 611:** **61 m high**, **4,801 m long**, *"one of the longest dams in the world"*, **8,100 Mm³ over 630 km²**, further dams at **Tikrapara** and **Naraj**, **1 million ha** in **Sambalpur, Bolangir, Puri, Cuttack**, **3.5 lakh kW**. ⚠️ Mean depth ≈ **12.9 m** — shallow for its area.
- ✅ **Bhakra, book-era Khullar folio 610:** reservoir **Gobind Sagar**; main canal **174 km**; gross command ~**27 lakh ha**; irrigation ~**15 lakh ha**; ❗ capacity **6.03 MAF (1963) → 5.5 MAF (1988)**, ❗ **6.03 − 5.5 = 0.53 MAF in 25 years** [N-18]. ❗ Worked fully at **L12**.
- ❌ **[N-11]:** Bhakra's *"highest straightway gravity dam in the world"* is **obsolete** — ❗ keep **518 m long and 226 m high** attributed, **drop the ranking**. ⚠️ **[U-05]** the DVC date *"February 18, 1948"* is **flagged, not adjudicated**.
- ❌ **[N-13] from Leong printed folio 70:** *"**Vetarna**, Vihar and Tulsi ... supply **Bombay**"* → **Vaitarna**, **Mumbai**; *"Okhla Reservoir supplies Delhi"*; the **Lloyd Barrage on the Indus at Sukkur**; **the Bhakra and Nangal Dams on the Sutlej**; and **the Red River Valley of Canada as the bed of former Lake Agassiz** — ❗ **a dead lake's floor is farmland**, exactly as with the Karewas.
- ❗ **The six discrimination tests:** **dendritic plan** · a **straight dam line** at the downstream end · **drowned hillslopes with no littoral shelf** and a drawdown ring · **the lake IS the river backed up** · **level follows operating rules** · **sited for demand and abutments**. ❗ **The tank is the hard case**: the giveaway is a **straight bund on the lower side only**.
- ❗ **[A7] The compressed life cycle:** a reservoir fills, stratifies, traps nearly all its sediment, loses **dead storage then live storage**, and ends as a weir on a sediment plain. ❗ **Leong's "draining and silting up", at engineering speed.** ✅ **Aliyar** appears independently at Majid printed **8.19**, Table 8.9, Tamil Nadu row [N-14 *"Aliayar"*]. ❗ **Named in the 2019 owned demand; key unavailable locally; no answer recorded or inferred.**
- ❗ **The taxonomy's closing prediction:** ❗ **the classes that die within a human lifetime are 5 and 6 — the two that make no hollow at all**, because both place a **barrier across a flow** and detain water in a shallow dish. ❗ Those are the lakes people live beside, and the lakes the syllabus clause is about.
- 🔁 **Routed out:** reservoir-and-state **matching** to `Geography/basic/36`; river regulation, basin transfer and interlinking to **Topic 05 (released, 20 lessons)**; irrigation policy, command-area development and canal engineering to the **economy and water-resources owners**; reservoir ecology and displacement to **Environment and Ecology**.

### Concept check

**Question:** A satellite image shows a water body with a many-armed branching outline occupying a valley system, a perfectly straight 4-kilometre edge at its lower end, hillslopes running directly into the water with no beach or reed fringe anywhere, a pale bare band twelve metres high above the present waterline, and an upper end that tapers imperceptibly into a river. A second image shows a small water body, roughly triangular, with a straight earth embankment along one side only and natural ground on the other two, set on a gentle slope in hard-rock country. Classify both, state which test is decisive in each case, and explain why both are expected to lose storage faster than a natural lake of comparable area.

**Model answer:** **The first is a reservoir, and four tests converge.** The **branching, many-armed outline** is decisive and is the test no natural process passes: it is the plan of a drowned valley system, water risen into the main valley and every tributary at once. No natural basin-forming process produces that shape — a rift gives a straight elongate plan, a cut-off a crescent, a crater or kettle a circle, a lagoon a coast-parallel strip. The **perfectly straight four-kilometre edge** is the dam, and straightness at that scale is never natural. The **absence of any littoral shelf, beach or reed fringe** confirms it: these margins are **valley sides that were dry land until recently**. The **twelve-metre pale band** is the drawdown zone, recording a level range driven by **operating rules** rather than by a budget. And the **upper end tapering into a river** shows the body **is the river, backed up**. **The second is a tank**, and only one test is decisive: a **straight earth embankment along one side only**, with natural ground elsewhere — Khullar's definition exactly, *"a bund of earth or stone thrown across a stream"*, partly dug out — sited on a gentle slope in hard-rock country, matching all four of his causes. **Both lose storage faster than a natural lake of comparable area**, for two reinforcing reasons. First, **trap efficiency**: both are barriers across flowing water, so velocity falls to near zero and essentially all delivered sediment is retained, whereas a tarn or kettle of the same area has a tiny catchment. Second, **both are shallow relative to their area**, because both drown a surface rather than occupying an excavated hollow — Hirakud holds 8,100 million cubic metres over 630 square kilometres on Khullar's book-era figures, a mean depth near 12.9 metres — so each unit of sediment removes a larger **proportion** of capacity. Bhakra is the measured proof: 6.03 million acre feet in 1963 to 5.5 in 1988.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat "artificial" as meaning "under control". The operator sets **releases** and nothing else. Sediment yield from the catchment, evaporation over hundreds of square kilometres, stratification in the deep section and the progressive loss of dead and then live storage are all ordinary physical geography operating on a basin that happens to have an engineered outlet.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "The artificial lake undergoes the same life cycle as the natural lake, compressed." Discuss with Indian evidence. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** A reservoir obeys the same ledger as any lake. It fills, stratifies in its deep section, and because velocity falls to near zero at the dam it traps almost all the sediment its river delivers, consuming dead storage and then live storage until the structure becomes a weir on a sediment plain. That is Leong's double process of draining and silting, at engineering speed. India supplies measured proof: Khullar records Bhakra's capacity falling from 6.03 million acre feet in 1963 to 5.5 in 1988, a loss of 0.53 in twenty-five years. Hirakud, in Odisha, impounds 8,100 million cubic metres over 630 square kilometres on the same book-era source, a mean depth near thirteen metres, so each unit of silt removes a large proportion of capacity. Tanks compress it further: the tank share fell from fourteen per cent in 1960-61 to about three in 2009-10.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** **3** for the six-class tree reproduced in order with its two-branch split between natural hollows and human or biological barriers; **4** for the natural-against-artificial discrimination method given as tests rather than as examples, with plan shape and the downstream edge both used; **3** for three Indian leaves correctly attached to different branches of the tree with their vintages attached. Deduct 2 for placing Hirakud outside Odisha; deduct 1 for any tank or capacity figure quoted without its source year.

## Lesson 9 — Limnology: stratification, turnover and the oxygen profile

Progress: L9 / 16 — Limnology: stratification, turnover and the oxygen profile  |  Stage: Advanced  |  Band: Standard

Canonical basis: Basic owner's **owned-core clause**, which names *"water budget, residence time, **stratification, turnover**, trophic state, eutrophication, succession and shrinkage"* as owned content; Basic owner's **process-control clause** — *"nutrients, flushing, **mixing** and decomposition govern **oxygen stress**"*; Basic owner §9.3, which supplies the decay-and-hypoxia chain — *"when the algae die, decomposition consumes dissolved oxygen; the resulting **hypoxia** kills fish and invertebrates."*
