### 4. 🇮🇳 The worked answer — Chilika plus Aral, built end to end

❗ **The canonical owner's §10.3 supplies two evidence units, each in the form claim → evidence → significance → limitation. The spine's step 3 requires two contrasting cases. This section builds the pair into a complete answer.**

```text
   EVIDENCE UNIT 1 -- ARAL  (canonical owner, section 10.3)
   CLAIM        an irrigation decision hundreds of kilometres
                upstream can destroy a fishery, a port function
                and a regional climate buffer
   EVIDENCE     diversion of the Amu Darya and Syr Darya for
                irrigation caused the Aral to fall, split and
                become hypersaline, stranding ports and ending
                its fishery
   DATED SLOT   book-era: level fell "by 13 m", area reduced to
                "two-thirds" (Majid pr. 6.32, Nystrom 2008)
                live: ~90 % of area and volume lost; bed is the
                ARALKUM; North Aral behind the KOK-ARAL DIKE
                (2005)  [secondary-sourced, 04 Oct 2026]
   SIGNIFICANCE water bodies are the TERMINAL ACCOUNTING POINT
                of basin-wide decisions
   LIMITATION   partial restoration of the northern remnant
                after inflow was retained shows the outcome was
                a POLICY CHOICE, NOT AN INEVITABILITY

   EVIDENCE UNIT 2 -- CHILIKA  (canonical owner, section 10.3)
   CLAIM        lagoons and lakes deliver services whose value
                is invisible until they fail
   EVIDENCE     Chilika, a brackish lagoon and an early Indian
                Ramsar site, sustains fisheries and migratory-
                bird habitat whose condition depends on the
                salinity regime set by its sea mouth
   DATED SLOT   ~1,165 km2 across Ganjam, Khurda and Puri;
                Irrawaddy dolphins reported at 159 in the 2026
                census, level with 2025  [secondary-sourced,
                04 Oct 2026; area varies with season and
                measurement convention]
   SIGNIFICANCE "conservation" of such a system is a
                HYDROLOGICAL MANAGEMENT PROBLEM, NOT A FENCING
                PROBLEM
   LIMITATION   salinity management trades fisheries against
                weed control and freshwater-dependent uses, so
                there is NO SINGLE OPTIMUM

   ** WHY THIS PAIR AND NOT ANY TWO CASES **
   -- one WORLD, one INDIAN
   -- one CLOSED basin, one SEMI-ENCLOSED coastal body
   -- one a FAILURE, one a PARTIAL RECOVERY
   -- one where the lever is UPSTREAM ALLOCATION, one where it
      is LOCAL MORPHOLOGY
   ===> the pair spans the whole argument space, so almost any
        15- or 20-mark lake question can be answered from it
```

### 5. The three-band routing, consolidated

❗ **The edition's final structural act is to state, in one table, what Topic 09 owns, what it shares and what it hands over.**

| Band | Count | ❗ Treatment |
|---|---:|---|
| ❗ **A. Owned** — six demands routed by exact filename to `Geography/basic/09_Lakes.md`, **all Prelims, none Mains** | **6** | ❗ Demand statement, the geographic reasoning tested, the lessons that build it, and the trap it punishes. ❗ **No option letter, no key, no elimination chain, anywhere.** |
| ⚠️ **B. Adjacent within Geography** | **4** | ⚠️ Named and routed by exact path; taught only to the extent Topic 09 supplies the physical substrate |
| 🔁 **C. Routed to Environment and Ecology** — **including all three Mains demands** | **9** | 🔁 Named and routed by exact path; used to show **what Topic 09 contributes and where it stops** |

❗ **The consequence, stated plainly as the edition's closing structural finding:** ⚠️ Topic 09 has the lane's **highest owned Prelims traffic and zero owned Mains traffic**, and the three real Mains demands on its subject matter belong next door. ❗ **An edition that annexed Ramsar law would be out of bounds and aimed at questions it does not own.** ❗ **An edition that ignored those demands would leave a candidate unable to write the physical half of an answer that needs it.** ❗ **The correct posture — supply the substrate, name the owner by path — is what Lessons 9, 10 and 15 do, and it is itself a scored skill.**

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | What it explains | What it does not explain |
|---|---|---|
| **The seven-step spine** | How to produce a structured 15-mark answer under time pressure without composing structure at the desk | The specific content of any question, which varies |
| **[A10] The four-question drill** | How to reason about an unfamiliar name; why inference beats recall on three of six owned demands | Any particular name's actual attributes |
| **The two evidence units** | Why a contrasting pair spans the argument space better than two similar cases | Cases outside that space, for which a third unit would be needed |

**Strongest objection.** ⚠️ Teaching a reusable spine produces formulaic answers. Examiners penalise templates, and a candidate who writes the same seven steps for every lake question will be marked down for not engaging with the specific demand.

**Reply.** ❗ The objection confuses a **skeleton** with a **script**. ⚠️ The spine specifies **what kind of thing goes where** — thesis, mechanism, two contrasting named cases, an effect chain, the Indian register, a balance, a graded conclusion — and specifies **nothing about content**; two answers built on it, one on saline lakes and one on urban wetlands, share no sentences at all. ⚠️ **Second**, the risk the objection names is handled by step 1: the **thesis is written to the question**, and a thesis written to the wrong question is immediately visible to the writer. ⚠️ **Third**, the spine is the **canonical owner's own**, independently recorded in the answer-worthiness audit, so using it is fidelity rather than invention. ❗ **Fourth**, at 250 words the structure costs about 230: a candidate composing structure at the desk will not finish.

⚠️ **Residual.** ⚠️ The spine is written for the **change** family, which is what the single mapped syllabus clause asks about. ❗ **It is not adapted here to a pure classification question**, for which the decoding table's first row is the guide and the Lesson 8 tree is the content. ❗ **No owned objective demand is worked anywhere in this edition**, and **no 2026 provisional key was consulted**.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** General Studies Paper I, the mapped change clause. ⚠️ **[U-01]** The world-process content of Lessons 3 to 8 is taught under that clause as the scaffolding of the change argument, and the mapping gap is **recorded, not corrected**.
- **Verified linkage:** ⚠️ **All six owned demands are built toward here and none is worked.** ❗ **Keys unavailable locally for five; provisional and unconsulted for the sixth; no answer recorded or inferred for any.** 🔁 The three Environment-owned Mains demands are the **answer targets this edition serves without annexing**, and each is routed by exact path.
- **Traps:** composing structure at the desk; narrating one lake's story for a *"discuss the shrinkage"* demand; giving a classification list without forming processes; confusing a lagoon with a freshwater lake; omitting the balance step at 15 or 20 marks; quoting an undated figure; drifting into Ramsar law in a Geography answer.
- **Answer use:** ⚠️ The whole lesson is answer use. ❗ **Open with the thesis, give the mechanism in one sentence, deploy two contrasting named cases, write the effects as a chain with arrows, add the Indian register, balance with the partial-recovery case, and conclude that the water body is a basin-scale accounting outcome rather than a local site.**

Revision notes:

- ❗ **The seven-step spine (canonical §10.2):** **[1] thesis** — *"rarely climatic alone ... diversion-and-abstraction outcome amplified by climatic variability"* · **[2] mechanism** — the closed-basin water budget and evaporative hypersensitivity · **[3] two contrasting named cases** · **[4] effect chain, with arrows** · **[5] the Indian register** · **[6] balance** · **[7] graded conclusion**.
- ❗ **The structure costs ~230 of 250 words.** ❗ **Fill slots; do not compose structure at the desk.**
- ✅ **Directive decoding:** *"classify by origin"* → **process-based genesis with a named example each**, never a bare list · *"discuss shrinkage and its effects"* → **budget failure plus the effect chain**, never one lake's story · *"why are lakes temporary"* → **infilling and outlet incision**, accelerated by humans · *"ecological value of wetlands and lagoons"* → services **with the trade-offs**, never confusing a lagoon with a freshwater lake.
- ⚠️ **Three generalising habits:** *"the role of"* asks for **proportion**; *"effects"* asks for a **chain with arrows**; **15 and 20 marks implicitly demand a qualification**, which is scored, not polite.
- ❗ **[A10] The four-question drill:** **where is the basin?** → **does it drain to the sea?** (the sill test) → **what made the hollow?** (read plan shape and setting) → **how deep for its area?** (origin predicts it). ❗ **Output: salinity, depth class, mixing regime, dominant death mode, which intervention works, and which topic owns the rest — without having heard the name.**
- ❗ **Plan-shape tells:** crescent in a floodplain = **ox-bow** · coast-parallel behind a sand ridge = **lagoon** · straight, elongate, very deep = **rift** · circular, high, in a cirque = **tarn** · circular, low, in outwash = **kettle** · circular in a flat plateau with a raised **overturned** rim = **impact** · ❗ **dendritic with a straight dam line = reservoir** · small with a **bund on one side only** = **tank**.
- ✅ **Leong's own drill, PDF 74, credited:** **Q1** sketch-map **Tanganyika, Great Salt Lake, Toba, Como, Victoria, Scutari, Mead** · **Q2** **ox-bow, kettle, crater, karst** formation with sketch maps · **Q3** five classes with three examples each plus three uses of man-made lakes · **Q4** rivers dammed for **hydro, flood control, irrigation, drinking water, transport** · **Q5** define **basin of inland drainage, tectonic lakes, haffs, beaver dams, playas, barrier lakes, tarns**.
- ❗ **India extension:** add **M3** (Himalayan), **M4** (peninsular, saline, lagoon) and **M5** (reservoir and tank) features; add the terms **polje, phumdi, hydroperiod, residence time, trap efficiency, dead storage, meromictic, interdeltaic depression**.
- ❗ **All thirteen previous-year-named water bodies appear in the edition's own map assets**, of which **nine are absent from the repository map bank**. ❗ **The bank is not edited in this lane.**
- ❗ **Evidence unit 1 — Aral:** claim, an upstream irrigation decision destroys a fishery, a port function and a climate buffer · evidence, Amu Darya and Syr Darya diversion · significance, water bodies are the **terminal accounting point of basin-wide decisions** · ❗ limitation, **partial restoration of the northern remnant shows the outcome was a policy choice, not an inevitability**.
- ❗ **Evidence unit 2 — Chilika:** claim, services are invisible until they fail · evidence, a brackish lagoon and early Ramsar site whose condition depends on the **salinity regime set by its sea mouth** · significance, ❗ conservation is a **hydrological management problem, not a fencing problem** · limitation, ❗ **no single optimum**, because salinity trades fisheries against weed control.
- ❗ **Why that pair:** one **world**, one **Indian**; one **closed basin**, one **semi-enclosed coastal body**; one **failure**, one **partial recovery**; one lever **upstream allocation**, one **local morphology**. ❗ **It spans the argument space.**
- ❗ **The three-band routing:** **6 owned** (all Prelims, none Mains — demand statements only, **no key, no option, no elimination**) · **4 adjacent within Geography** · **9 routed to Environment and Ecology, including all three Mains demands**.
- ❗ **The closing structural finding:** ❗ **supply the substrate, name the owner by path.** ❗ Annexing is out of bounds; ignoring leaves the candidate unable to write the physical half.

### Concept check

**Question:** You are given fifteen minutes and 250 words for: *"Examine the changes affecting India's inland and coastal water bodies and their consequences."* Set out, step by step, how you would construct the answer before writing a sentence of it — naming the structure you would use, what goes in each slot, which two cases you would deploy and why those two, and the single most common way this answer fails.

**Model answer:** **I would not compose structure at the desk; I would fill a skeleton I already hold.** The canonical seven-step spine is written for this family, and at 250 words the structure costs about 230, leaving roughly twenty words of slack. **Step one, thesis, about twenty-five words, written to this question:** change in water bodies is rarely climatic alone but an abstraction, conversion and sediment outcome amplified by climatic variability. Writing it first is also the self-check — a thesis that does not fit is immediately visible. **Step two, mechanism, thirty words:** the budget, with outlet status deciding salinity and residence time deciding whether the body exports or accumulates. **Step three, two contrasting cases, thirty-five words:** **Chilika** and the **Rajasthan closed belt**. Chilika is semi-enclosed and coastal, its salinity set by inlet conveyance, its trajectory a **partial recovery** achieved by a **morphological** intervention. The Rajasthan belt is **closed, shallow and evaporation-dominated**, its trajectory a loss, its only lever **upstream inflow**. Opposite in enclosure, in direction of change and in where the lever sits, so together they span the argument space. **Step four, the effect chain, forty words, written with arrows**: area or depth loss, salinity or nutrient change, fishery and habitat loss, exposed bed and dust, loss of climatic moderation, livelihood loss — marking where I stop, since species and statute are Environment's. **Step five, the Indian register, thirty-five words**, including the point that India has no Aral because its major rivers are exorheic. **Step six, balance, thirty-five words**, with the northern Aral and mouth management, and the genuine trade-offs. **Step seven, a graded conclusion, thirty words.** **The commonest failure is step four collapsing into step three** — narrating one lake at length and never reaching the chain, the balance or the conclusion.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat a reusable spine as a template. A template fixes sentences; a spine fixes **slots**. Two answers built on this one — on Rajasthan's saline lakes and on Bengaluru's urban tanks — would share no sentence at all, because every slot is filled from the question. The formulaic answer the examiner penalises is the one with fixed content, not the one with a disciplined structure.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "Changes in India's water bodies are better explained as basin-scale accounting outcomes than as local degradation." Discuss. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** A water body is the terminal point of its catchment's accounts. Its storage changes as inflow, precipitation and groundwater input minus outflow, evaporation, seepage and abstraction, so every upstream decision on diversion, land cover and sediment yield is registered inside it. Rajasthan's saline lakes are closed, shallow and evaporation-dominated, with streams off the western Aravalli drying in the sands and the Ghaggar lost near Hanumangarh after about 465 kilometres; any inflow reduction can only be absorbed by shrinking. Khullar records Bhakra's capacity falling from 6.03 million acre feet in 1963 to 5.5 in 1988, a catchment-erosion outcome registered as lost storage. Chilika qualifies the thesis: its salinity depends on inlet conveyance, so a local morphological intervention at the mouth restored it. The conclusion is therefore graded — most change is basin-scale and demands upstream allocation, but coastal lagoons retain a genuinely local lever in their mouth geometry.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** **5** for the spine reproduced **in order** — thesis, mechanism, contrasting cases, effect chain, Indian register, balance, graded conclusion — with the order itself visible; **3** for two **independent** evidence units deployed, differing in enclosure and in direction of change; **2** for the directive correctly decoded, with *"discuss"* producing a qualified argument rather than a narration. Deduct 2 for narrating a single lake's story; deduct 1 for omitting the balance step.

# VERIFIED PYQ LINKAGE AND ANSWER APPROACHES

## The structural anomaly, stated once more because the whole edition rests on it

❗ A sweep of **sixteen** central verified previous-year ledgers across **2018 to 2026**, using a Topic-09 term pattern closed by **exact owner filename**, returns **twenty-one genuine topic-relevant hits** and **six exact-owner hits**. ❗ **All six owned demands are objective Prelims demands. Owned Mains demands across 2018-2026: zero.** ⚠️ Three lexical false positives were identified and removed, and are disclosed so the sweep is reproducible: *"par**aral**lelogram"* in a 2019 aptitude item, removed by word-boundary tightening; *"**Bhoj**pur"* in a 2023 General Studies Paper I item; *"**superior**ity"* in a 2021 General Studies Paper III item; and *"tanks"* meaning military vehicles in a 2026 Prelims item.

❗ **Meanwhile three genuine Mains demands on wetlands and water bodies exist, and all three are routed to Environment and Ecology.** ❗ This edition is therefore built as the **physical and hydrological substrate** for those answers and **hands the institutional, legal and biodiversity depth off by exact file path**.

## Answer-neutrality, binding and non-negotiable

❗ Every owned demand below is **objective**, and in every case the key is either **unavailable locally** or **provisional**. Therefore, throughout this file:

1. ❗ **No option letter is stated, implied, hinted or converged upon** for any owned demand.
2. ❗ **No elimination chain** that leaves exactly one surviving option appears.
3. ❗ **No phrasing** that asserts a correct pairing, marks one statement as uniquely true, or declares an option right or wrong appears in connection with any owned demand.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

⚠️ Four checks. Three consolidate an arc; the fourth spans the file. ❗ **No compiled objective corpus appears here or anywhere in this edition.**

### Cumulative check A — the budget and genesis arc, Lessons 1 to 8

**Question:** Build the chain from "a hollow exists" to "this lake will die in the following way and on the following timescale", naming every intermediate step, and identify the two points at which the chain forks.

**Model answer:** **Step one: the hollow's origin fixes its vertical scale.** Tectonic subsidence has no base level and reaches kilometres; glacial overdeepening is self-limiting at hundreds of metres; caldera collapse is limited by the evacuated magma volume; solution stops at the base of the soluble bed and chokes on its own residue; deflation stops at the water table; fluvial cut-off is limited to a channel's depth; and a barrier impounds only what the valley or bay already held. **Step two: origin also fixes plan and area**, independently of depth. **Step three: depth and area give volume, and volume over outflow gives residence time.** **Step four: mean depth gives the area-to-volume ratio, its reciprocal** — halve the depth and every area-driven effect doubles: evaporation, heat gain, nutrient concentration, proportional sediment impact, light at the bed, wind at the bed. **Step five is the first fork: is there an exit for dissolved solids, by any route?** If yes, the lake exports salt, holds a self-correcting level, and its threat is **sedimentary**. If no, salt accumulates monotonically, salinity rises by a double ratchet, and the threat is **hydrological**. Karst proves the condition was correctly stated, since a polje leaks solids downward and stays fresh. **Step six: residence time and mixing regime set the internal chemistry** — a deep lake stratifies and releases iron-bound phosphate from anoxic sediment; a shallow one is polymictic and recycles continuously. **Step seven is the second fork: which clock reaches zero first?** Mode A when the basin is closed, arid, shallow and abstracted; mode B when the catchment-to-lake ratio is high; neither when the catchment is tiny and the volume enormous. **Step eight: timescale follows from class** — and the two classes that **make no hollow at all**, depositional and artificial, die within a human lifetime.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the two forks as independent. Outlet status influences which clock dominates, but it does not switch the other off: a closed basin still receives sediment, and an open basin still loses water. Every lake runs both clocks simultaneously, and the mode of death is only ever a statement about which one is running faster.

### Cumulative check B — the correction-and-date arc, across the whole file

**Question:** This edition carries eighteen corrections. State the single procedure common to all of them, give four instances drawn from four different source types, and explain why the procedure is worth more to a candidate than any individual correction.

**Model answer:** **The procedure has four steps: state the source's claim with its attribution; state the superseding evidence with its own identifier and date; name precisely what changed; and never delete either side silently.** **Instance one, an inter-source contradiction.** Leong calls Tanganyika *"the world's deepest lake"*; Majid states *"Baikal ... is the deepest lake in the world ... depth 1743 m"*; the canonical owner is hedged as *"among the deepest"*, so the hedge governs and Baikal is stated as deepest with its figure dated. **Instance two, an obsolete statistic.** Majid's Aral entry, on 2008-era atlas data, gives a thirteen-metre fall and reduction to two-thirds; secondary sources retrieved on 4 October 2026 report roughly ninety per cent of area and volume lost, the bed now the Aralkum, the North Aral stabilised behind the Kok-Aral dike of 2005. **Both are shown with their dates**, because together they measure the rate of decline. **Instance three, a superseded inference.** Majid's printed 3.31 reports a radio-carbon epidemic conclusion for Roopkund; Harney and colleagues in *Nature Communications* (2019) 10:3670 report thirty-eight individuals in three genetically distinct groups, two separated by roughly a millennium. **Instance four, an unsupported superlative inside the canonical owner itself.** The Advanced owner calls Sasthamkotta *"Kerala's largest freshwater lake"* where its own cited book says only *"a large fresh water lake"*; nothing contradicts it, so it is **dropped as unsupported** and no replacement claimant is named. **Why the procedure outranks any instance.** It **transfers** to the next stale figure a candidate meets; it is **itself examinable**, because citing a figure with its vintage demonstrates discipline no confident number can; it **protects against the opposite failure** of deleting a dated baseline that is itself evidence about change; and it enforces the canonical caution to describe the mechanism and the direction of change.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat a correction as a reason to distrust the source wholesale. Leong is the only systematic genetic classification of lakes in this edition's entire book corpus and is decisive for Lessons 3 to 8; three superlatives being wrong does not impeach the taxonomy. Correct the specific claim, keep the structure, and say which is which.

### Cumulative check C — the India application arc, Lessons 13 to 15

**Question:** Explain why a single national lake-restoration specification, applied uniformly across India, would succeed in some places, be redundant in others and be actively counter-productive in at least one, using the families established in this file.

**Model answer:** **Four Indian families with four dominant mechanisms make a uniform specification incoherent.** **Where it would succeed: the urban lake.** Dal, Bellandur, Varthur and Powai suffer **cultural eutrophication**, and the canonical ladder is matched to it — intercept and treat the inflow, remove nutrient-rich sediment, restore buffer and catchment, and attempt biological restoration only then. Interception addresses the flow; de-sedimentation addresses the stock already banked in the mud, which is why cutting sewage alone shows nothing for years. **Where it would be redundant: the high Himalayan lake.** A tarn has a catchment barely larger than itself, cold water and a bedrock floor; interception has nothing to intercept and de-sedimentation nothing to remove. What matters there is the **dam**, and that hazard chain belongs to the glaciation topic. **Where it would be counter-productive: the lagoon and the floating-wetland system.** For a lagoon the controlling variable is **inlet conveyance against river discharge**, so the intervention is morphological, and a freshwater quality target would destroy the brackish regime the fishery and bird habitat depend on — the canonical limitation being that **there is no single optimum**. Worse is Loktak, where the damage came from **stabilising the level**: phumdis survive by grounding during the annual low, and any specification treating stable level as an objective would reproduce the injury. **Where it would miss the mechanism: the closed saline lake.** Rajasthan's belt is closed, shallow and evaporation-dominated; salinity is structural, not a pollution outcome, and recovery **requires restoring inflow, not local measures** — an upstream allocation decision outside any lake-side specification. **The general finding** is that the correct unit of intervention is the **basin**, and the correct question is always which mechanism is dominant here.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not read "restoration" as a single activity with variable intensity. The four families fail by different mechanisms, so their repairs are not stronger or weaker versions of one another but **different interventions altogether**, and applying the wrong one is not merely wasteful — for a lagoon or a phumdi system it is the injury.

### Cumulative check D — the whole file

**Question:** This topic has the lane's highest owned Prelims traffic and zero owned Mains traffic, and the three real Mains demands on its subject matter belong to another subject. Justify the design of this edition against that fact, and state what would have made it fail.

**Model answer:** **The design follows from reading the ledger honestly.** Six owned demands, all objective, five with no key available locally and one with a provisional key that was not consulted; three Mains demands on wetlands and water bodies, every one routed to Environment and Ecology. Two responses are available and both are wrong. **The first is to work the objective demands** — to decide what each probably wanted and teach toward it. That converts an edition into key-inference on items whose answers are unavailable, and it is forbidden by the owner's rule that unavailable or provisional keys are not invented. The edition therefore states each demand, names the **capability** it tests, names the lessons that build it, and stops. **The second is to annex the Mains demands** — to teach Ramsar criteria, wise use, the Wetlands Rules and programme detail so the edition appears to cover the questions that carry marks. That is out of bounds under the canonical boundary clause and is aimed at questions this topic does not own. **The design adopted is to build the substrate and route the rest by exact path.** Topic 09 supplies what no Environment file does: the four hydrological functions and the finding that retention consumes the asset providing it; the point that reclamation removes depth and volume before area, so limnological collapse precedes visible disappearance; lake degradation and urban flooding as two symptoms of one failure; the volatility of a designation count and the fact that a count is not a condition; and the hydroperiod mechanism behind Loktak. **Four things would have made it fail:** leakage on an owned stem; annexation of wetland policy; taxonomy bloat, defused by closing every genesis lesson on a change consequence; and stale quantification, defused by dating every book figure against a dated live position. **The deliverable is a method, not coverage.**

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not infer from "zero owned Mains demands" that this topic cannot be examined at Mains level. The single mapped syllabus clause is a change clause and is a General Studies Paper I clause; a question on changes in water bodies and their effects would land here directly. The absence in the ledger is a record of what has been asked, not a prediction of what can be.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

❗ **Every item here is authored for this edition and is therefore answerable in full.** ❗ **No previous-year demand is answered anywhere in this file.**

## 10-mark practice (about 150 words each)

**Q1.** "A lake's genesis predicts how it will die." Examine. *(Examine — 10 marks, about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** Genesis fixes depth and plan, and those fix vulnerability. Tectonic subsidence has no base level, so rift lakes are kilometres deep with very low area-to-volume ratios; they outlast institutions, but long residence makes them accumulate whatever enters. Glacial overdeepening is self-limiting at hundreds of metres, so tarns persist on organic infill while morainic-dammed lakes can fail suddenly, their dams being unconsolidated debris. Erosional basins are shallow because each has a limiting floor — the base of the soluble bed, or the water table where damp grains turn cohesive. Depositional and artificial lakes make no hollow at all; they place a barrier across a flow and detain water in a shallow dish, so both failure routes operate at full strength and they die within a human lifetime. The qualification is that budget overrides structure where a basin is closed.

**Q2.** "Outlet status, not climate, determines whether a lake becomes saline." Discuss. *(Discuss — 10 marks, about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** Run water and salt budgets separately and the point is unavoidable. Evaporation is a large water output but contributes exactly zero salt output, so in a basin with no exit for dissolved solids the salt mass has an input and no sink and rises monotonically, while volume falls — a double ratchet ending in evaporite precipitation. Climate enters only as a rate. A closed basin in a humid climate still accumulates salt, more slowly, while a through-flowing lake in a hyper-arid desert stays fresh because water leaves as water. The karst case sharpens the rule: polje lakes are closed at the surface yet fresh, because dissolution built conduits that export solids downward. India illustrates both sides — Rajasthan's closed belt is saline, Wular on the Jhelum is fresh — and the Ladakh lakes show altitude is irrelevant.

**Q3.** "Urban lake degradation and urban flooding are two symptoms of one failure." Examine. *(Examine — 10 marks, about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** Both follow from converting a storage-and-infiltration landscape into a sealed, piped, encroached conveyance system. Sealing the catchment removes infiltration, so runoff arrives fast and dirty: inflow becomes flashy, with high peaks and little baseflow, and the baseflow was the lake's dry-season supply. Encroachment of inflow channels severs the connection that would flush the water body, while sewage is piped in directly, so loading rises as flushing falls. Simultaneously the lake's flood-buffering function depends on free storage above its normal level; filling, encroachment and keeping it full all remove that headroom. The result is one failure with two faces — a lake receiving concentrated nutrients with no capacity to export them, and a city whose detention storage has gone. Attenuation, note, only redistributes a flood peak; it does not reduce its volume.

**Q4.** "Artificial lakes are not exempt from the life cycle of natural ones." Discuss with Indian evidence. *(Discuss — 10 marks, about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** A reservoir obeys the same ledger as any lake. It fills, stratifies in its deep section, and because velocity collapses at the dam it traps nearly all its river's sediment, consuming dead storage below the lowest outlet before live storage above it — which is why the loss is invisible while the structure still fills and delivers. Khullar records Bhakra's capacity falling from 6.03 million acre feet in 1963 to 5.5 in 1988, a loss of 0.53 in twenty-five years. Hirakud, in Odisha, holds 8,100 million cubic metres over 630 square kilometres on the same book-era source, a mean depth near thirteen metres, so each unit of silt removes a large proportion of capacity. Tanks compress it further: the tank share fell from fourteen per cent in 1960-61 to about three by 2009-10.

## 15-mark practice (about 250 words)

**Q5.** "Changes in inland water bodies are basin-scale accounting outcomes rather than local degradation events. Examine, with reference to one world and one Indian case, and state what follows for protection." *(Examine — 15 marks, about 250 words.)*

**Model (about 250 words):** A water body is its catchment's terminal accounting point: storage changes as inflow, precipitation and groundwater input minus outflow, evaporation, seepage and abstraction, so every upstream decision on diversion, land cover and sediment yield registers inside it. A closed basin has no outlet to throttle, so reduced inflow is absorbed only by shrinking until evaporation from the smaller surface matches it; being flat-floored, a small level fall strips a large area, and shrinking makes it shallower, so loss accelerates as salinity rises by a double ratchet.

The world case is the Aral. Its feeders, the Amu Darya and Syr Darya, were diverted from the 1960s for large-scale cotton irrigation in a high-evaporation basin. Majid's 2008-era account records a thirteen-metre fall and reduction to two-thirds of original size; secondary sources retrieved in October 2026 report roughly ninety per cent of area and volume lost, the exposed bed now the Aralkum. Effects ran beyond the shoreline: hypersalinity ended the fishery, ports were stranded, the saline agrochemical-laden bed became a dust source degrading farmland, and local climatic moderation weakened.

The Indian case, Chilika, qualifies the thesis: its salinity depends on inlet conveyance against river discharge, so a morphological intervention at the mouth restored it, and a 2026 census reported its Irrawaddy dolphins level with 2025.

What follows is graded. Most change demands upstream allocation; but the northern Aral stabilised behind the Kok-Aral dike of 2005, and a lagoon retains a genuinely local lever. The outcome is a policy choice, not an inevitability.

## 20-mark practice (about 250 words)

**Q6.** "Every lake is simultaneously running a hydrological clock and a sedimentary clock. Discuss the proposition, adjudicate it for two contrasting Indian settings, and state what each implies for intervention." *(Discuss — 20 marks, about 250 words.)*

**Model (about 250 words):** The proposition is sound and is the topic's organising argument. Hydrological death occurs when inflow persistently falls short of outflow, evaporation and abstraction: the level falls, area contracts on a flat floor, salinity concentrates and the ecosystem collapses. Sedimentary death occurs because velocity collapses on entry, so the basin traps nearly all incoming sediment, consuming dead storage first and then live storage while mean depth falls, light reaches the bed, macrophytes colonise and trap more sediment. Neither clock can be stopped; the mode of death is whichever reaches zero first. The discriminator is sharp: in hydrological death the level falls; in sedimentary death the level is fine and the capacity is gone.

Adjudicating two Indian settings. In Rajasthan's closed belt the hydrological clock dominates. Khullar records that streams off the western Aravalli dry in the sands and the Ghaggar is lost near Hanumangarh after about 465 kilometres, so there is no through-drainage; the lakes are shallow, with Sambhar ranging from a few centimetres to about three metres between seasons on Majid's book-era account. In the Sutlej and Mahanadi reservoirs the sedimentary clock dominates: Khullar records Bhakra falling from 6.03 million acre feet in 1963 to 5.5 in 1988.

The implications diverge. A closed basin recovers only by restoring inflow, which is an upstream allocation decision, often outside its own jurisdiction, and no in-lake measure substitutes. A silting reservoir needs catchment land management, which reduces the rate but cannot recover lost volume; desilting removes the stock but must be repeated indefinitely.

```text
     | advancing          | shaded out         | rising salinity    |

     | by excavation)     | flow + remove      | (restore INFLOW,   |
     |                    | stock)             | not local measures)|
 fail| desilt without     | AERATE or DE-WEED  | treat the lake     |
     | catchment control  | without stopping   | instead of the     |
     |                    | the nutrient source| catchment          |
     +--------------------+--------------------+--------------------+
   ** All three are reported as "the lake is dying". The canonical
      terminology control requires all three to be separated. **
```

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

❗ **The final teaching section. All sixteen lessons condensed, under topic-specific headings, after all teaching and all practice.**

## A. The ledger and the sill — what governs a lake (L1-L2)

- **Lake** = a hollow on land holding standing water with a **free surface**. ❗ Classify by the **dominant basin-forming process**, not by water quality.
- **Budget:** `dS/dt = (Qi + P + Gi) − (Qo + E + Go + A)`. ❗ `Qo` is decisive by its **presence or absence**; `E` scales with **area not volume**, is a **distillation**, and is indifferent to the lake's state; `A` has **no negative feedback**.
- **[A1] Residence time `T = V/Q`** — short → flushes and recovers; long → integrates salt, nutrients, metals and heat. ⚠️ A whole-lake average that hides short-circuiting. ❗ **No `T` is computed for any Indian lake here.**
- **The sill test** decides exorheic against endorheic. ❗ **Geometry, not climate.**
- **[A2] Salt mass balance:** evaporation gives a large water output and **zero** salt output → salt accumulates **monotonically**; volume falls → **double ratchet** → evaporites precipitate as carbonates, then gypsum, then halite.
- ❗ The condition is **no exit for dissolved solids by any route** — proved by karst, closed at the surface and fresh.
- **[A4] Flat-floor multiplier:** area lost ≈ depth change ÷ floor gradient. ⚠️ Shallow → high area-to-volume → more evaporation, faster warming, full mixing, faster concentration.
- 🇮🇳 **Wular** (Jhelum, through-flowing, fresh) against **Sambhar** (closed, evaporation-dominated, saline; a few centimetres to about 3 m, Majid printed 3.31, book-era).
- 🇮🇳 ✅ **Khullar folios 112-113:** inland drainage in **Aksai Chin** and the **Rajasthan desert**; the **Ghaggar** lost near **Hanumangarh** after ~**465 km**, formerly an **Indus affluent**; western-Aravalli streams **dry in the sands**.
- ✅ **Leong's governing sentence:** lakes *"are only temporary features ... eliminated by the double process of draining and silting up."*

## B. The six genetic classes and the inference they support (L3-L8)

- **[A3] Depth is a function of origin, not size.** Tectonic **kilometres** · glacial **hundreds of metres**, self-limiting at the reverse slope · caldera **hundreds** · solution and deflation **tens** · cut-off and barrier **metres**. ❗ **Area and depth are independent.**
- **1 Earth movement:** warping · faulting · **half-graben** (deep, narrow, long, **asymmetric**) · full graben · downwarp (vast, shallow). ✅ East African rift *"3,000 miles"* ≈ 4,800 km; Caspian *"143,550 sq miles"* ≈ 371,700 km². ❌ **[N-02] Baikal deepest (Majid: 1743 m)**, not Tanganyika. ❌ **[N-03] Titicaca = highest navigable.** ❌ **[N-04] "Rudolf" → Turkana.** ❌ **[N-15] never lift a figure from the scrambled Table 6.19 text layer.** 🇮🇳 **Wular**; the **Karewas** as a dead lake's floor.
- **2 Glaciation, six forms:** **tarn** (cirque, bedrock, rock lip) · **ribbon** (overdeepened trough) · **morainic-dammed** (❗ **fails suddenly** — 🔁 GLOF to **T06**) · **kettle** (buried ice melts out of outwash; ❗ the tarn's opposite) · **drift-deposition** (deranged drainage) · **rock-hollow** (ice-sheet scour; ✅ Finland *"over 35,000"* — **[U-04] threshold-dependent**). 🇮🇳 ✅ **Tsongmo**, the source's own *"glaciated tarn lake"*, ~3,780 m.
- **3 Volcanic and impact:** **crater** (material out; constructional rim) · **caldera** (roof **in**; diameter set by the chamber; truncated rim; later cone on the floor) · **lava-dammed** (a **rock** dam, durable; **[U-08]** Sea of Galilee attribution contested) · **subsidence**. ❗ **Impact is a separate canonical row**: **no cone, no vent**, a hollow in a **pre-existing flat surface**, a rim of **overturned target rock**. 🇮🇳 **Lonar, Buldhana** — impact in basalt; ❗ its saline alkaline water is a **budget outcome**.
- **4 Erosion:** dissolution is **self-focusing** → a conduit network → ❗ **a solution basin is a sieve by construction**. **Polje:** flat clay floor, swallow holes of **finite capacity** → ❗ **floods wet, dries dry; that alternation is normal**; its real death is **conduit enlargement**. **Deflation** stops at the **water table**, so hollows are **shallow and predisposed to hold water**. ❗ **No named Indian solution lake exists in this evidence base.**
- **5 Deposition:** **ox-bow** — migrate, narrow, breach, ❗ **clay plugs at both ends**, which is the lake-forming step; crescent; ❗ **lives on episodic overbank reconnection**, which also fills it. **Lagoon** — ❗ **[A8] the inlet is the whole argument**; salinity = inlet conveyance against river discharge; ❗ **the lever is morphological, the result ecological**. **Barrier lake** — ✅ *"short-lived"*, failing by **positive-feedback notch incision or piping**; ❗ contrast a **rock outlet, which is a negative feedback**.
- **6 Human and biological:** reservoir · **tank** (✅ *"a bund of earth or stone thrown across a stream"*) · mining · ornamental · beaver. ❌ **[N-01] Hirakud is in ODISHA**, on Khullar folio 611, Majid printed 8.19 and the map bank. ❗ **Six discrimination tests:** dendritic plan · straight dam line · drowned hillslopes with no shelf · the lake **is** the river backed up · level follows operating rules · sited for demand and abutments; ❗ **the tank's giveaway is a bund on one side only**.
- ✅ **Khullar tanks, book-era:** ~5 lakh big and 50 lakh small; ~1.64 Mha; **14 % (1960-61) → ~3.0 % (2009-10)**; **AP with Telangana 727 thousand ha ≈ 28.8 %**; **TN 589 thousand ha ≈ 23 %, ~24,000 tanks**; 2009-10 sources **wells 62 %, canals 26 %, tanks 3 %, other 9 %**. ❗ **[U-06] counts threshold-dependent; [U-07] the ambiguous state-share sentence omitted.**
- ❗ **The taxonomy's closing prediction:** classes **5 and 6** — the two that make **no hollow** — are the ones that die within a human lifetime.

## C. Limnology and the two terminologies (L9-L10)

- **Three layers:** **epilimnion** (warm, lit, wind-mixed, oxygen **source**) · **metalimnion / thermocline** (❗ a **density gradient**, not a temperature) · **hypolimnion** (cold, dark, sealed, oxygen **sink only**).
- ❗ **Salinity stratification is stronger than thermal** → **meromictic**, permanently anoxic at depth. ⚠️ Fresh water is densest at ~4 °C, so cold-winter lakes stratify **twice**.
- ❗ **Turnover:** uniform temperature erases the density difference → full mixing. ❗ It **re-oxygenates the bed AND lifts accumulated nutrients into the light** — hence blooms **after** turnover. ⚠️ Regimes: dimictic · monomictic · ❗ **polymictic (most Indian lakes)** · meromictic.
- ❗ **Internal loading:** anoxia → ferric iron reduced to soluble ferrous → **iron-bound phosphate released** → mixed up at turnover → a bloom from nutrients that arrived **years ago**. ❗ **The lake has a phosphorus bank in its mud.**
- ❗ **[N-08] Split the term.** **Natural eutrophication / succession**: sediment-driven, millennial, consumes **volume**, **irreversible**. **Cultural eutrophication**: nutrient-driven, decadal, consumes **oxygen**, **partly reversible**. ❗ The local glossary merges them four ways.
- ❗ **Separate three things:** succession (space) · cultural eutrophication (oxygen) · water-budget shrinkage (water). ❗ **Ask what is actually being lost.**
- ✅ **Restoration ladder:** intercept and treat the inflow → remove nutrient-rich sediment → restore buffer and catchment → **only then** biological restoration. ❗ ✅ *"Aeration or de-weeding without stopping the nutrient source is a recurring and expensive failure."* 🔑 **Source before symptom; stock before flow.**
- 🇮🇳 **Dal** (deep, seasonal, internal loading) · **Bellandur and Varthur** (shallow tanks, polymictic; ❗ **a tank cascade now distributing load instead of surplus**) · **Powai** (❗ artificial origin confers no immunity). ❗ **Froth = surfactants and organic load; fire = combustible floating material** — water-quality observations, **not** limnology, and **not** single-cause.

## D. The two clocks (L11-L12)

- **Mode A, hydrological:** inflow < outflow persistently → **level falls → area contracts → salinity concentrates → ecosystem collapses**. ❗ A closed lake's **only adjustment is to shrink**; the process is **positive-feedback**; **splitting is a threshold**.
- ✅ **Effect chain:** area loss → salinity or nutrient change → fishery and biodiversity collapse → **exposed bed, dust and salt export** → **loss of local climatic moderation** → livelihood loss → migration and, where institutions are weak, conflict. ❗ **Four links owned, three routed.**
- ❌ **[N-05] Aral:** book-era (Majid printed 6.32, *Nystrom* 2008) **13 m fall, "two-thirds" of original size, 28,500 km²**; 📰 live, secondary, **04 Oct 2026**, ~**90 %** of area and volume lost, bed = **Aralkum**, eastern South Aral lobe dry, North Aral behind the **Kok-Aral dike (2005)**, ~2,800 km² (2006) → ~3,400 (2020). ❗ **Both shown, both dated.**
- ❗ **The strongest concluding line:** ✅ *"partial restoration of the northern remnant after inflow was retained shows the outcome was a **policy choice, not an inevitability**."* ❗ Two sub-basins, **one climate**, opposite trajectories.
- **Mode B, sedimentary:** catchment erosion → ❗ **velocity collapse → trap efficiency ≈ 1** → delta builds from the tail → ❗ **dead storage consumed first, invisibly** → sediment passes the lowest outlet → **live storage falls as a step** → shallowing → **macrophytes colonise and trap more** → marsh → dry land.
- 🇮🇳 ✅ **Bhakra, Khullar folio 610:** **6.03 MAF (1963) → 5.5 MAF (1988)**; ❗ **6.03 − 5.5 = 0.53 MAF**, ~**8.8 %** of the stated 1963 capacity, in **25 years**. ❗ **No rate, extrapolation or current capacity stated anywhere.** Reservoir **Gobind Sagar**; canal **174 km**; command ~**27 lakh ha**; irrigation ~**15 lakh ha**. ❌ **[N-11]** the *"highest straightway gravity dam"* ranking **dropped**; **518 m × 226 m** retained. ⚠️ **[U-05]** the DVC date **flagged**.
- 🇮🇳 ✅ **Hirakud, Khullar folio 611:** **ODISHA**, **61 m × 4,801 m**, **8,100 Mm³ over 630 km²** (⚠️ mean depth ≈ **12.9 m**), **1 Mha**, **3.5 lakh kW**. ✅ **Kosi:** **1,149 m × 72 m** barrage at **Hanumannagar, Nepal, 1965**; canal **43.5 km**; **5.19 lakh ha**; **27.6 km** embankments; **1954** agreement revised **1961**.
- ❌ **[N-06] Chad:** book-era *"10,360 to 25,900 sq km"*, area **16,300 km²** (*Nystrom* 2008); 📰 live, secondary, **04 Oct 2026**, ~**90-95 %** loss since the 1960s, recent area ~**1,350-1,700 km²**, season-dependent; **Transaqua** at feasibility stage 🔁 (T05). ❗ **For Chad, "the area" is a distribution.** ❗ ✅ Aral *"diversion-dominated"* against Chad *"rainfall decline + abstraction + population pressure"* — ❗ **the pair blocks generalisation; no attribution share asserted.**
- ❗ **[A5] The discriminator:** ❗ **in mode A the level falls; in mode B the level is fine and the capacity is gone.**

## E. India's lake geography (L13-L14)

- **Three Himalayan bands:** above ~3,000 m **glacial** (✅ Gangabal, Sheshnag, Kailash-Kund, Sanasar; ✅ Tsongmo) · 1,500-3,000 m ❗ **compound** (✅ Naini *"tectonic/structural ... modified by slope processes"*; ✅ Sat Tal *"complex"*, seven lakes near **Bhimtal, ~1,370 m**) · valley floors **structural lows on rivers** (**Wular**, **Dal**, the **Karewas**).
- ❗ **Altitude is a genetic argument only above the former snowline.** ❗ **Altitude does not make a lake fresh; an outlet does** — **Pangong Tso** and **Tso Moriri** are high, elongate, **closed and saline**.
- ✅ **Gazetteer tail, Majid printed 3.31:** **Pushkar** — ❗ *"a dam across the Luni headwaters"*, hence **artificial** · **Renuka**, Sirmaur, ❗ **no genetic label in the source** · **Suraj Tal**, below the **Baralacha Pass, ~4,980 m**, source of the **Bhaga**.
- ❌ **[N-09] Roopkund:** ✅ ~600 skeletons, ~**5,030 m**, discovered **1942**; the *"epidemic"* claim is **superseded** by **Harney et al., *Nature Communications* (2019) 10:3670** — **three genetically distinct groups**, ~23 South-Asian-related near **800 CE**, ~14 eastern-Mediterranean-related near **1800 CE**, one Southeast-Asian-related. ❗ Used **only** as the date-discipline example.
- **Peninsular and western families:** ❗ **no Peninsular glacial lakes** · the **Rajasthan endorheic saline belt** · the **east-coast lagoon chain** · the **hard-rock tank country** · isolated specials.
- ✅ **Sambhar, book-era:** ~**70 km west of Jaipur**, *"the largest salt lake of India"*, **5 km stone dam**, salt *"for over a thousand years"*, **a few cm to ~3 m**, a **Ramsar site**. ❗ Three readings: two states not a mean · a **millennial salt industry proves the ratchet** · a **Class 6 overlay on a natural saline lake**.
- 🇮🇳 **Chilika:** ✅ Asia's largest brackish lagoon, **India's first Ramsar site (1981)**, barrier-enclosed, **mouth actively managed**; 📰 secondary, **04 Oct 2026**, ~**1,165 km²** across **Ganjam, Khurda, Puri**, **159 Irrawaddy dolphins in the 2026 census, level with 2025** — ❗ used **only** as evidence the restored hydrology holds. ✅ *"conservation ... is a **hydrological management problem, not a fencing problem**"*, with ✅ **no single optimum**.
- ❗ **Pulicat is EAST coast** · **Vembanad** is the **west-coast backwater** · ❗ **Kolleru is NOT a lagoon** but *"**between the Krishna-Godavari deltas**"* · **Kanwar / Kabar Tal** is the **north Bihar ox-bow**.
- ❌ **[N-10]** the **"Kerala's largest freshwater lake"** superlative is **unsupported by the cited source and is dropped**; ✅ the book says only *"a large fresh water lake"* in Kollam district. 🔑 **Carry a superlative only with its source and criterion.**

## F. Wetlands, bounded, and the answer architecture (L15-L16)

- ❗ **Wetland ≠ lake.** A wetland is land where the water table stands at, near or above the surface long enough to control soil and vegetation; ❗ **hydroperiod** is its defining variable. ❗ **Every lake is on a trajectory toward becoming one.**
- ❗ **Four owned functions:** **storage** (area × surcharge) · **recharge** (time is the control) · **attenuation** (❗ **detention — the hydrograph is flattened, not reduced in volume**; requires **free storage**; the benefit is **downstream**) · **retention** (❗ the function **consumes the asset providing it**). ❗ **Of eleven functions and services, four owned, one partly, six routed to four Environment files by path.**
- 📰 ✅ **PIB 1913469, 03 APR 2023, re-retrieved 04 Oct 2026:** *"**2,31,195 wetlands** (at 1:50000 scales and area >= 2.25 ha) ... **15.98 million hectare** including rivers and excluding paddy field areas ... around **4.86%** of the geographic area."* ⚠️ Check: 15.98 ÷ 0.0486 = **328.8 mha**. ❗ **[U-09] the Atlas is labelled both "2017" and "released ... in February 2022"; both carried.**
- 📰 ✅ **Decadal change:** **+18,810** wetlands and **+0.64 Mha** over **2006-07 to 2017-18** — ❗ **not unambiguous good news**: substantially **inland man-made**, while natural coastal wetlands declined. ❗ **A count is not a condition.**
- 📰 ✅ **[CA10] The frame, bounded:** **Wetlands (Conservation and Management) Rules, 2017** under the **Environment (Protection) Act, 1986**; **National Wetlands Committee**; **State and UT Wetlands Authorities**; **Integrated Management Plans**; **NPCA**. ❗ **Existence and function only.**
- ❗ **[N-17] Re-verified 04 October 2026:** PIB **2294036**, **03 AUG 2026** — **Glaw Lake**, Kamlang Tiger Reserve and WLS, **Arunachal Pradesh**, India's **101st** and the **state's first**; *"from **26 ... in 2014** to **101 today**"*. ❗ **[CA4]** against **75** sites / **1.33 Mha** in the April 2023 release — ❗ **always quote a count WITH its date.**
- ❗ **Terminology:** **Ramsar designation** is an **international listing of ecological importance** under the **1971** convention, **not** a national protected-area notification. **The Montreux Record** is an **early-warning register**, **not** a funding or heritage list. ⚠️ **[U-10] secondary, 04 Oct 2026:** Loktak and Keoladeo reported as India's entries; Chilika reported **removed in 2002**; `ramsar.org` **HTTP 403** on re-attempt. ✅ **Wular is not on the Montreux Record; Loktak is.**
- ❗ **The Ithai chain:** a **phumdi** is a **floating** mat that survives by **grounding in the annual low**; a **barrage stabilises the level**; phumdis stop grounding, thin and shrink. ❗ **The wetland is damaged by removing a FLUCTUATION, not by removing water.**
- ❗ **The seven-step spine:** thesis → mechanism → **two contrasting named cases** → effect chain with arrows → the Indian register → balance → graded conclusion. ❗ **~230 of 250 words; fill slots, do not compose structure.**
- ❗ **[A10] The four-question drill:** where is the basin? → does it drain to the sea? → what made the hollow? → how deep for its area? ❗ **Output: salinity, depth class, mixing regime, death mode, the workable intervention, and which topic owns the rest — without the name.**
- ❗ **Two evidence units:** **Aral** (diversion → *"terminal accounting point"* → ❗ *"a policy choice, not an inevitability"*) and **Chilika** (mouth-set salinity → *"hydrological management, not fencing"* → ❗ *"no single optimum"*). ❗ **One world, one Indian; one closed, one semi-enclosed; one failure, one partial recovery; one upstream lever, one local lever.**
- ✅ **Leong's PDF 74 drill, credited:** sketch-map **Tanganyika, Great Salt Lake, Toba, Como, Victoria, Scutari, Mead**; formation of **ox-bow, kettle, crater, karst**; five classes with three examples each; rivers dammed for five purposes; define **basin of inland drainage, tectonic lakes, haffs, beaver dams, playas, barrier lakes, tarns**. ❗ Extend with **M3, M4, M5** and with **polje, phumdi, hydroperiod, residence time, trap efficiency, dead storage, meromictic, interdeltaic depression**.

## Page conventions, recorded so no citation here is mistaken for another edition's pagination

- **Leong:** Chapter 9 "Lakes" is local scan **PDF 69 to 74 inclusive**; PDF 68 is the tail of Chapter 8 and **PDF 75 opens "Chapter 10 Coastal Landforms"**. Printed folios are legible only on even pages: **PDF 70 shows printed 67**, **PDF 72 shows printed 69**, giving `printed = PDF − 3`, verified twice. ❗ **PDF 69, 71, 73 and 74 have the folio cropped out and are cited by PDF page only.** ❗ **[U-15] PDF 72's left margin is shaved by about one character; its content is reported attributively and no complete sentence from it is quoted.**
- **Khullar:** the folio offset **drifts** across the volume — PDF 73 shows **112/113**, PDF 316 shows **594/595**, PDF 324 shows **610/611**. ❗ **Citations are given as `printed folio NN (local scan PDF p.N)` with the folio read off the render, never computed.**
- **Majid *Geography of India*:** the local copy is an **every-fifth-page selective scan**, verified PDF 62→3.21, 63→3.26, 64→3.31, 65→3.36, 66→3.41. ❗ **Pagination is chapter-relative and no folio may be computed.** ❗ **[U-14] printed 3.22-3.25 and 3.27-3.30 are physically absent**, which is exactly where *"Types of Lakes 3.27"* and the opening of *"Main Lakes of India 3.28"* sit.
- **Majid *Indian and World Geography*:** the only book with a text layer. ❗ **[N-15] Table 6.19's name and area columns are scrambled in that layer; every area quoted in this edition was read off the rendered page image** and is cited as *The Nystrom Desk Atlas*, 2008, reproduced in Majid, printed 6.30-6.32.

## Sourcing constraints, disclosed rather than concealed

1. ❗ **Leong PDF 69-74 is the only systematic genetic classification of lakes in the entire local book corpus.** ⚠️ Mitigated by cross-checking every class against C1's independent origin table.
2. ❗ **Majid *Indian and World Geography* has no dedicated lake chapter**; lakes appear only incidentally plus a world gazetteer.
3. ❗ **Khullar has no Lakes section anywhere in its contents**, verified on rendered contents pages.
4. ❗ **Majid *Geography of India* is missing the four printed pages carrying the India lake typology.** ⚠️ The typology here is reconstructed from canonical Markdown plus the surviving gazetteer tail at printed 3.31.
5. ❗ **No limnology section exists in any local book**; Lesson 9 is built on the canonical owned-core and process-control clauses plus derivation, and says so.
6. ❗ **Qdrant was probed, found unavailable and treated as non-blocking; it does not appear in the manifest.**
